# Farsight — Knight Hacks IX competitor and placement audit

Captured: 2026-10-11T19:56:13+00:00. Read-only scout. Rankings are estimates.

Farsight is a credible finalist-style project, not a comfortable favorite. My base-case overall rank is **9th of 212**, with a plausible **5th–15th** band if the integrated demo is stable. The strongest sponsor opportunity is **NVIDIA: about 5th, with a 2nd–8th band**. These are subjective ordinal forecasts, not official results or calibrated probabilities.

The nearest architecture rival is **Understory**. The hardest checkable robotics rival is **Permanence**. **Here**, **Apprentice**, **Mimisis**, **Atlas**, **VIGIL**, and **HearthWatch** raise the bar on impact, focused demonstrations, custom engineering or evidence. Farsight’s best differentiator is combining live rescue telepresence with a spatial review record that links reconstructed rooms and person sightings back to real images.

## Coverage and method

Audited all 9 gallery pages: 24 projects on each of pages 1–8 and 20 on page 9, totaling 212 unique URLs. Page 9’s next control points to “#”, establishing the end of pagination. Read all gallery cards; retrieved and skimmed 134 plausible or same-track project pages, all HTTP 200. Every one of the 114 unique entrants in the four requested sponsor pools is included, plus 20 other plausible/general contenders. The remaining 78 received card screening only.

Track membership comes from the event’s exact sponsor filters, paginated to the end: Hugging Face 78 (4 pages), Full Sail 36 (2), NVIDIA 21 (1), Encompass 43 (2). These are opt-in counts, not proof of eligibility, judging attendance, or winners. The public project pages’ “Submitted to” sections show the event, not a complete per-prize list; Farsight’s story separately names all four tracks. I therefore use the filtered galleries for verified prize membership and do not pretend it was visible under every project’s “Submitted to”. A second pass also paginated all 19 sponsor-prize filters, so the census and dossiers include every publicly filter-visible sponsor opt-in, not only the four ranked pools. General prizes do not require a separate sponsor opt-in.

Evidence is first-party Devpost prose, embedded/linked demo availability and published metrics. I did not independently replay every video, inspect rival repositories, operate rival demos or observe judging. Numbers attributed to teams are self-reported. A video link is not proof that every claimed feature works. This is a complete gallery/target-track census and a public-evidence ranking audit, not a physical validation audit.

The live event rubric is Originality, Technical Understanding, Functionality, Design and Wow Factor. I combine those with impact and exact sponsor fit; no official numerical weights are published on that page. Integrated functioning and narrow checkable claims are preferred to long feature lists. Order beyond the leading ~15 is especially uncertain; the complete lists provide triage, not meaningful one-place precision. Entry age, likes, comments and page position are not scoring inputs. No official winners were used or inferred.

Official event and rubric: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/
Gallery: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?utf8=%E2%9C%93

## Farsight baseline and reliability

Published baseline: ELEGOO rover, iPhone ARKit RGB/depth/pose, onboard Jetson Orin Nano with YOLO11n-pose/TensorRT, navigation integration, cellular/Tailscale transport, Quest/WebXR operator control, local Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct/Whisper/Piper, and Mac/Metal Brush reconstruction with face-linked browser review. The page describes immersive Quest splat walking, but bringing the browser’s face-evidence interaction into the immersive viewer is still a next step.

Timing must stay bounded: Farsight reports 114 seconds for a **saved 60-second, 119-photo sample**, including 112 seconds of Brush training and 62 seconds of CPU face processing in parallel. A 10-second idle allowance/local-copy stand-in gives 124 seconds, **excluding real Jetson transfer and browser loading**; the sample reuses a longer run’s LiDAR seed. It is not a demonstrated live one-minute-drive-to-ready-room result.

The brief flags rover power instability during demos as a concern. I did not independently observe that failure, its frequency or a repaired state. Treat it as a supplied operational concern, not a measured audit result. If the rover resets or cannot move during judging, the base-case 9th could fall roughly into the 15th–30th range, with sponsor podium chances materially worse. That downside band is judgment, not an empirical distribution. Other teams also report power, calibration or integration failures, notably Understory; apply the same proof standard to both sides.

[Farsight](https://devpost.com/software/farsight-t4qyli)

## Overall top ten

1. **[Permanence](https://devpost.com/software/permanence)** — A real shell-game reveal tests 3D memory directly; reported 7 recorded and 3 live successes plus custom quantization make a formidable rival.
2. **[Here](https://devpost.com/software/here-vqlrhg)** — Spatial captions, directional haptics and a measured open-model bakeoff create a strong accessibility story and concrete sensing loop.
3. **[Apprentice](https://devpost.com/software/apprentice-0owzs7)** — One camera grades human and robot builds with the same rules; a complete, understandable feedback loop and measured limits.
4. **[Mimisis](https://devpost.com/software/mimisis)** — Stereo neural hand tracking drives a real arm; reported latency and pick-and-place results make the hard robotics work easy to assess.
5. **[Understory](https://devpost.com/software/understory-gj3in2)** — The closest architecture rival: VR survey rover, SLAM, Nav2 and open plant models; staged surveys and past power/network problems temper confidence.
6. **[Atlas](https://devpost.com/software/atlas-q4wypt)** — Jetson memory, cited answers and multiple connectors have quantified soak and throughput claims; training on AMD is separated from edge runtime.
7. **[VIGIL](https://devpost.com/software/vigil-1fx8gp)** — Custom flight electronics and live thermal detection are strong SAR hardware; stable hover and camera tests are not an integrated autonomous search flight.
8. **[Swarm](https://devpost.com/software/swarm-oqg6l4)** — Custom PPO swarms, GPU simulation and held-out comparisons offer deep technical work with an excellent serious-training story.
9. **[Farsight](https://devpost.com/software/farsight-t4qyli)** — VR rover control, onboard perception and local voice plus LiDAR-seeded rooms have exceptional breadth; live reliability and demo focus decide the result.
10. **[HearthWatch](https://devpost.com/software/hearthwatch-0bkwp4)** — A trained posture model on a 2 GB Nano connects to nurse response; reported locked-test metrics and live 13 FPS strengthen execution evidence.

Honorable threats: [OmniBelt](https://devpost.com/software/omnibelt), [Waymaker](https://devpost.com/software/veil-8xerbq), [Blurr](https://devpost.com/software/blurr-lgyc03), [Jolteon](https://devpost.com/software/jolteon), [BlackBox](https://devpost.com/software/blackbox-s1qdc4), [Doorway](https://devpost.com/software/doorway-0eysjv), [Glint](https://devpost.com/software/nuggies). fpga_LangModel is a specialist technical threat (complete custom neural inference on an FPGA); Fobo, He Can See Us and the other polished software/game entries can outperform this forecast on design or presentation. Their lower position here is uncertainty, not evidence of inferior execution.

## Sponsor ranking cards

### NVIDIA: Farsight ~5/21; plausible 2–8; 3 prize positions

Best podium opportunity, but a crowded edge-robotics field. Permanence has an exceptionally checkable reveal; Understory and Apprentice have focused physical feedback loops; Atlas reports strong soak evidence. Mac splat training is not Jetson computation.

Judge emphasis: Point to the onboard Orin Nano, then show camera → TensorRT person detection → motor/navigation behavior and a local spoken answer. Say exactly what runs on the phone, Jetson and Mac. Show actual onboard throughput under the demonstrated workload if a receipt is available.

Exact membership census: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?prize_filter%5Bprizes%5D%5B%5D=107095&page=1

Full ordering (all opted-in entrants; lower positions have weak confidence):

1. [Permanence](https://devpost.com/software/permanence) — A real shell-game reveal tests 3D memory directly; reported 7 recorded and 3 live successes plus custom quantization make a formidable rival.
2. [Understory](https://devpost.com/software/understory-gj3in2) — The closest architecture rival: VR survey rover, SLAM, Nav2 and open plant models; staged surveys and past power/network problems temper confidence.
3. [Apprentice](https://devpost.com/software/apprentice-0owzs7) — One camera grades human and robot builds with the same rules; a complete, understandable feedback loop and measured limits.
4. [Atlas](https://devpost.com/software/atlas-q4wypt) — Jetson memory, cited answers and multiple connectors have quantified soak and throughput claims; training on AMD is separated from edge runtime.
5. [Farsight](https://devpost.com/software/farsight-t4qyli) — VR rover control, onboard perception and local voice plus LiDAR-seeded rooms have exceptional breadth; live reliability and demo focus decide the result.
6. [HearthWatch](https://devpost.com/software/hearthwatch-0bkwp4) — A trained posture model on a 2 GB Nano connects to nurse response; reported locked-test metrics and live 13 FPS strengthen execution evidence.
7. [OmniBelt](https://devpost.com/software/omnibelt) — Custom belt and ring integrate depth, object memory and last-meter guidance; substantial sensor fusion with a clear wearer interaction.
8. [Waymaker](https://devpost.com/software/veil-8xerbq) — Offline Jetson vision, ultrasonic haptics and local Q&A have a clear wearer benefit; loose wiring and broad testing remain limitations.
9. [Fireline](https://devpost.com/software/fireline-764nir) — Jetson smoke detection and real weather sensing feed small alert packets; the satellite link and most fleet units are simulated.
10. [ThirdEye](https://devpost.com/software/third-eye-mzrl95) — Persistent edge event tracking is meaningful Jetson work; a fixed-camera workflow has less mechanical breadth but may demo more reliably.
11. [Glint](https://devpost.com/software/nuggies) — Live left/right EOG selection and deliberate blink-to-speak provide a compelling assistive demo; electrode drift constrains robustness.
12. [Knight-Wagon](https://devpost.com/software/knight-wagon) — Sophisticated multi-sensor delivery stack; the full delivery chain is reported in simulation, with a smaller proven physical layer.
13. [Guiding Hands](https://devpost.com/software/guiding-hands) — Projected rehabilitation exercises and Jetson hand tracking have clear utility; fuller robot integration is still future work.
14. [WaveBoard](https://devpost.com/software/waveboard-7y94fk) — Jetson hand tracking turns a projector into a teaching surface; easy to demonstrate, but less autonomous reasoning.
15. [Dottie](https://devpost.com/software/gerald-2hb7v3) — A physical Braille cell and learner-data pipeline are compelling; next steps still include object-to-cell and teaching integration.
16. [StoryForge](https://devpost.com/software/storyforge-x78pfk) — Local interactive story generation with a child’s drawing is engaging; actual on-Jetson throughput and scene consistency matter. The opt-in alone does not establish central GPU use.
17. [A.D.A: Autonomous Device Assistant](https://devpost.com/software/a-d-a-autonomous-device-assistant) — Ambitious self-improving arm and synthetic-data pipeline; the author admits calibration makes practical manipulation difficult.
18. [Sophro Shoppers](https://devpost.com/software/shopping-cart-yvkgd2) — A Jetson camera cart connects product recognition to dietary/budget help; recognition and complete checkout reliability are uncertain.
19. [B.O.B. (Building-Site Observation Bot)](https://devpost.com/software/bob-pjfhcx) — Construction rover perception is directly relevant; the page explicitly leaves autonomous driving and full sensor wiring/testing for later.
20. [InterviewOS](https://devpost.com/software/interviewos-ft05ag) — Adaptive interview automation is useful, but less distinctive than embodied perception; live Jetson contribution needs to be made explicit. The opt-in alone does not establish central GPU use.
21. [In-cube-ator](https://devpost.com/software/in-cub-ator) — Moisture sensors turn watering into a physical game; creative and tangible, but less computational breadth. The opt-in alone does not establish central GPU use.

### Encompass: Farsight ~7/43; plausible 3–12; 3 prize positions

Excellent capture-to-review story; custom sensing rivals are formidable. Here, VIGIL, Pulse and AquaWave can showcase unusual sensor hardware; Farsight mainly integrates commercial sensing. Its advantage is useful spatial evidence, not inventing LiDAR.

Judge emphasis: Show synchronized measured RGB, LiDAR depth and pose becoming a real person observation, a face pin, and the original boxed frame. The strongest sentence is: “This pin opens the actual image that created it.” Explain measured geometry versus learned splats.

Exact membership census: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?prize_filter%5Bprizes%5D%5B%5D=107096&page=1

### Hugging Face: Farsight ~7/78; plausible 3–12; 3 prize positions

Strong open-model application; outside the base-case podium. Here has a model bakeoff; Permanence has custom quantization; Understory uses specialist BioCLIP. Farsight needs task-specific results, not just a model list.

Judge emphasis: Make the Hub artifacts and their jobs concrete: Qwen3-VL sees, Whisper hears, Piper speaks. Demonstrate one view-grounded question on the Jetson. Explain the local/privacy value and memory scheduling, then show the reconstruction/evidence workflow. Do not count YOLO branding alone as meaningful Hugging Face integration.

Exact membership census: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?prize_filter%5Bprizes%5D%5B%5D=107093&page=1

### Full Sail: Farsight ~6/36; plausible 3–12; 1 prize positions

Most vulnerable fit: the sponsor asks for a serious game or training simulation. Swarm, KYU-BOT, HearthWatch and Hold Short make simulation/training central. A teleoperated rover alone is a weaker fit. CrowdClear has strong teaching structure but admits physical Quest testing and fuller pathfinding are future work.

Judge emphasis: Present a responder training goal, the drill, how pathfinding and face detection affect decisions, and the resulting debrief. Show timed drill scoring only if it actually runs. Use the walkable room and sighting evidence to explain what a trainee learns.

Exact membership census: https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?prize_filter%5Bprizes%5D%5B%5D=107094&page=1

## Closest direct competitors

- **[Understory](https://devpost.com/software/understory-gj3in2)** — The closest architecture rival: VR survey rover, SLAM, Nav2 and open plant models; staged surveys and past power/network problems temper confidence. Target tracks: Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[Permanence](https://devpost.com/software/permanence)** — A real shell-game reveal tests 3D memory directly; reported 7 recorded and 3 live successes plus custom quantization make a formidable rival. Target tracks: Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[VIGIL](https://devpost.com/software/vigil-1fx8gp)** — Custom flight electronics and live thermal detection are strong SAR hardware; stable hover and camera tests are not an integrated autonomous search flight. Target tracks: Hugging Face, Encompass.
- **[R2 Hope](https://devpost.com/software/r2-hope)** — A physical triage rover combines patient search, lidar steering and eye monitoring; medical and generalized navigation claims remain prototype-level. Target tracks: Encompass.
- **[B.O.B. (Building-Site Observation Bot)](https://devpost.com/software/bob-pjfhcx)** — Construction rover perception is directly relevant; the page explicitly leaves autonomous driving and full sensor wiring/testing for later. Target tracks: NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[ARCHER Program](https://devpost.com/software/archer-program)** — Rescue drone coordination overlaps the mission, but the public story offers less compact measurement evidence than VIGIL. Target tracks: Encompass.
- **[Mimisis](https://devpost.com/software/mimisis)** — Stereo neural hand tracking drives a real arm; reported latency and pick-and-place results make the hard robotics work easy to assess. Target tracks: Hugging Face, Full Sail.
- **[OmniBelt](https://devpost.com/software/omnibelt)** — Custom belt and ring integrate depth, object memory and last-meter guidance; substantial sensor fusion with a clear wearer interaction. Target tracks: Hugging Face, Full Sail, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[Waymaker](https://devpost.com/software/veil-8xerbq)** — Offline Jetson vision, ultrasonic haptics and local Q&A have a clear wearer benefit; loose wiring and broad testing remain limitations. Target tracks: Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[Atlas](https://devpost.com/software/atlas-q4wypt)** — Jetson memory, cited answers and multiple connectors have quantified soak and throughput claims; training on AMD is separated from edge runtime. Target tracks: Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[ThirdEye](https://devpost.com/software/third-eye-mzrl95)** — Persistent edge event tracking is meaningful Jetson work; a fixed-camera workflow has less mechanical breadth but may demo more reliably. Target tracks: Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass.
- **[Doorway](https://devpost.com/software/doorway-0eysjv)** — Offline indoor AR combines pose, walls and sign reading; a strong spatial-computing rival if relocalization works live. Target tracks: Hugging Face, Full Sail, Encompass.
- **[Beacon](https://devpost.com/software/recall-bou9l6)** — Face memory, saved frames and spatial guidance overlap Farsight’s evidence story; recognition reliability needs careful demonstration. Target tracks: Hugging Face, Full Sail.

## Strengths

- **Breadth with a coherent mission:** A responder drives, finds a person, asks about the view, then reviews a spatial record. The components can tell one story rather than compete for attention.
- **Measured spatial capture plus learned appearance:** LiDAR/pose and original images can ground the room and sightings; the splat adds visual context. Understory’s public roadmap still puts full Gaussian-splat sites in future work.
- **Useful provenance:** A face pin opening its actual boxed camera frame is more persuasive than an unexplained map dot or a generated medical note.
- **Substantial edge integration:** Vision, navigation, streaming, motor commands and local voice share a constrained onboard device; explain the real partition clearly.

## Weaknesses

- **Reliability can erase breadth:** Functionality explicitly asks whether the project reliably works end to end. Power instability or a long restart can outweigh several impressive subsystems.
- **No equally compact truth test:** Permanence can hide a ball and reveal the answer. Farsight needs an equally obvious person → location → original-frame demonstration.
- **Reconstruction timing is narrower than a headline:** The saved-sample build is useful engineering evidence, but not a live end-to-end turnaround receipt.
- **Limited field validation:** No verified responder study, broad SAR localization test, repeated physical mission benchmark or clinical validation was established in this audit.
- **Full Sail fit needs a training outcome:** VR presence and Nav2 alone do not make a serious training simulation. Demonstrate an actual learner task and debrief; describe unshown scoring as unverified.
- **Open AI is integrated, not uniquely trained:** Several rivals contribute model evaluation, custom quantization, training or specialist pipelines; model-name breadth alone will not win Hugging Face.

## Recommendation

Use one short, continuous mission in the pitch: show the rover and its onboard Jetson; drive to a person; show the live detection and a grounded local answer; open that sighting in the room and click through to the original image. If reconstruction is prebuilt, label it as a recorded run and state the bounded timing. Present only the portions that work in the available demo. Show a repeatable live movement/perception slice before adding optional features. This is a presentation recommendation, not a request to change control behavior or add runtime checks.

Lead NVIDIA with onboard compute, Encompass with capture-to-evidence, Hugging Face with the models’ concrete roles and observed usefulness, and Full Sail with the trainee’s goal and debrief. Do not tell every room the same six-minute feature list. No external submission, post, like, comment, code change, push or PR was made by this audit.

## Evidence and reproducibility

Commands used: `chrome-devtools-axi --help`, `CHROME_DEVTOOLS_AXI_SESSION=farsight-rivals CHROME_DEVTOOLS_AXI_AUTO_CONNECT=1 chrome-devtools-axi pages` (failed: no DevToolsActivePort), read-only Harness rediscovery/health calls, then `chrome-devtools-axi stop`. AXI could not discover the live endpoint; the maintained attach skill permits Harness fallback. Existing Chrome was healthy and was not restarted. `browser-harness` listed tabs, created exactly one background task tab (05471FB9F9CAA8A8BE8FF114A47AD4A8), navigated only that tab and used read-only browser `fetch` plus DOM parsing. A 5-second bulk-evaluation timeout was corrected with a 60-second response budget; all 134 pages subsequently captured successfully. No form submissions were made.

Capture files inside the disposable worktree: `data/farsight-rivals/gallery.json` (9 pages/212 unique cards), `tracks.json` (four paginated membership censuses), `projects.json` (134 HTTP-200 page texts and tech/video links), `all-sponsors.json` (all 19 paginated sponsor censuses), `event.txt` (live prize counts and rubric). `build_report.py` contains the exact ordinal order and reasoning. Because worktree files can disappear, all 212 card summaries and all 134 retrieved project dossiers follow below; the portable HTML source is appended to the surviving report.

Source guide and design references: `docs/hackers-guide.md` (local track-purpose summary), `quest/src/style.css:1` (dark system font and muted blue/green palette). Current live Devpost confirms three prizes for HF/NVIDIA/Encompass and one for Full Sail; credit amounts should not be confused with the page’s generic “cash” labels. No runtime safety or behavior checks were added.

We asked ourselves: What if AI could conduct personalized initial round interviews at scale, without requiring a human interviewer to be present? This could allow companies to conduct a way larger amount of first round interviews where they can choose from a greater pool of candidates where this time they know a lot more about them then their resumes. ### [AuditPilot — Internal Audit Policy Navigator](https://devpost.com/software/auditpilot-internal-audit-policy-navigator)

Card: AuditPilot — Internal Audit Policy Navigator Bring your policies home — get answers without sending your documents to external AI services. Your policies. Your machine. Your answers. Private, local, and built for trust. 0 0

Tech tags: python. Hardware mentions: quest.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does AuditPilot answers plain-English questions about an Accounts Payable policy. It finds the most relevant parts of the policy, writes an answer using only those parts, and says when the answer is not in the document. Users can download the full policy from the app to check the answer themselves. How I built it Extract: read the 19-page policy PDF with PyMuPDF and removed the repeated page headers. Chunk: split the text into chunks of 1200 characters with 150 overlap, using LangChain's RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter. Embed and index: turn each chunk into a vector with the BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 embedding model and store them in a FAISS index. Retrieve: embed the question the same way and fetch the 3 closest chunks. By default, FAISS ranks chunks by distance, where a smaller distance means a closer match: $$d(\mathbf{q}, \mathbf{c}) = \lVert \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{c} \rVert_2$

Story evidence excerpt: What it does The Oracle predicts your odds of winning, the category you stand the best chance in, and tries to provide helpful advice so that you can alter your fate before it is too late. How we built it The Oracle is trained on 3,526 projects studied across 17 in person hackathons within the last 3 years. The Oracle judges a project's chances of winning using a two stage classifier. A text model estimates how "winner-like" the project write up is. Then a model combines that score with project completeness and technology features gathered from the DevPost. Challenges we ran into We lost a lot of time coming up with ideas and didn't start until the second day. This put us on a pretty substantial time crunch and taught us the importance of preparing some ideas before hand. With this, a person can order from a phone, browse 18 UCF campus shops and their menus in the iOS app, build a cart, and check out with the current location becoming the drop-off point. The whole point is that the robot comes to the customer. The order goes to the robot, which drives itself along sidewalks using cameras, depth, lidar, and GPS. It stops for obstacles and people, and treats crosswalks carefully. Live tracking is also available, following the robot on a map in real time, with order status from "placed" through "on the way", "arrived," and "delivered". Card: Paralassist Helping paralyzed patients communicate when their voice cannot 4 0

Tech tags: css, html, javascript, python, typescript. Hardware mentions: quest, EEG.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, Databricks: Best Use of Databricks. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Paralassist uses an electroencephalogram (EEG) with a visual stimulus to convert a user's brain signals into messages. Users gaze at flickering tiles associated with messages, and generative AI is used to bring meaning to their responses. How we built it Visual Frequencies: The human brain will attempt to replicate visual frequencies from flashing lights with Steady-State Visually Evoked Potentials (SSVEPs). We use these to associate them with meaningful messages and allow users to communicate with a glance. EEG Acquisition: Electrodes placed at the occipital lobe receive SSVEPs that are emitted when the user is exposed to specific visual frequencies associated with messages. AI Interpreters: Two AI models, a TRCA model and a model using the EEGNet neural network, calibrate themselves to the user’s brain waves and then interpret their SSVEPs into meaningful messages or co

### [BlackBox](https://devpost.com/software/blackbox-s1qdc4)

Card: BlackBox It’s a black box for your car that runs on your phone: it detects a crash, saves the evidence, and reconstructs what happened in 3D within seconds. 0 0

Tech tags: arduino, ffmpeg, javascript, node.js, opencv, python, react, three.js, typescript, webgl. Hardware mentions: LiDAR, Arduino Nano, camera.

Sponsor opt-ins: [MLH] Best Use of ElevenLabs, Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, Full Sail: Pathways & Perception, A Serious Games & Simulation Challenge, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, BNY: Financial Services Challenge, OneEthos: Innovation for Good, Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does The crash re-enactor turns the phone already in your car into a crash black box. The phone keeps a rolling buffer of motion data and video. When it detects an impact, it saves the 10 seconds before and the 5 seconds after, checks whether the driver is okay, and can alert an emergency contact. Seconds later, our web app shows a 3D replay of the crash with the video synced to the sensor data, plus a report with the impact force, direction and speed change. An AI model describes the scene from the video and writes the report narrative. Every number is labelled as an estimate, and the report never assigns fault. It presents what was recorded. How we built it The capture side is a web page that uses the phone's motion sensors and camera, so there is no app to install. You scan a QR code and tap Start. The web app is built with React, TypeScript, Vite and Tailwind, with the 3D

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of The full chain works on real hardware. Crash the car, and a replay and report appear on the laptop about 20 seconds later, then on the hosted site. We lost our core hardware mid-hackathon and still shipped something better than the original plan. What we learned Build the simulator first. Because our pipeline ran on simulated crashes from the start, losing the Arduino cost us a sensor and not the project. We also learned that constraints improve designs. We would not have chosen the phone if the Nano had kept working, and the phone was the right answer. What's next for BlackBox A native app with faster sensor access for better measurements, signed records so a report can be shown to be unaltered, and reconstruction of both vehicles when both are running it. Built With arduinoclaudedevicemotion-apiffmpegfigmagemmajavascriptmediarecordermoondreamnode.jsollamaopencvpythonreactreact-three-fibersupabasetailwindcssthree.jstypescriptvercelvitewebgl Submitted to Knight Hacks IX Created by Christian Puello Mayank Kotla Upadhyay-Yash465 Upadhyay

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Our hardware died halfway through. The original design was a sensor pod built on an Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense, mounted on a Lego car and streaming data over Bluetooth. It worked, and we had live data flowing into the app. Then midway through the hackathon the Nano stopped being detected by our computers entirely. Nothing we tried brought it back, and we had no spare. That failure made the project better. We asked what else has an accelerometer, a gyroscope, a camera and a radio, and the answer was in our pockets. Moving the black box onto a phone gave us video, which the Arduino never could, and that made the AI scene description and the synced replay possible. It also meant the product went from a device you would have to manufacture to a link anyone can open. We also dropped two other approaches along the way, an overhead tracking camera and phone LiDAR scanning, when they turned out not to fit. And we learned that phone sensors are slow compared to how fast a real crash happens, which is why we are upfront that our numbers are estimates. Accomplishments 

What's next evidence: What's next for BlackBox A native app with faster sensor access for better measurements, signed records so a report can be shown to be unaltered, and reconstruction of both vehicles when both are running it. Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of The turn-around: someone speaks behind you, the belt buzzes, you turn, and their caption is waiting on them. A hand-built belt a stranger can put on and understand in seconds. An AI layer with a real safety rule: models can escalate, never suppress. A full system that runs without any of its hardware, from fixtures and mocks, with one message contract shared across Python, Swift, and TypeScript. What we learned Direction is the missing half of accessibility for sound. A label without a bearing leaves the user searching. Haptics should carry direction and nothing else. Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Two motors didn't survive. We designed for eight motors and coin motor leads are hair-thin. We ended up with six working channels, remapped the firmware, added the bitmask command so two neighbors can fire together to point between them, and re-tuned the direction mapping. Three models, one GPU. JEV-9B is 18 GB at full precision; the laptop has 16 GB shared with speech and sound. 4-bit got it to ~6 GB. Cold, it missed our latency gate (496 ms). Warm, it passed (208 ms). Speech models hallucinate on noise. In a loud hall the transcriber wrote sentences nobody said. Now we only trust a segment if the array heard a talker long enough, and drop segments the model itself doubts. Campus Wi-Fi. It blocks Postgres and isolates devices from each other, which is exactly why the relay and HTTPS database driver exist. Four ideas of "forward." The array, the compass, the camera's field of view, and the belt all disagreed. Nothing worked until everything became a bearing in one shared frame. Overloading the user. Story evidence excerpt: What it does Mimesis is an end-to-end, vision-based teleoperation system for the SO-ARM101. It works as follows: Hand Tracking: Two consumer webcams and the WiLoR neural network find 21 keypoints on each hand. We triangulate them into a 3D hand pose. Two-Hand Control: The right hand moves the gripper through 3D space. Twisting the left hand rotates the wrist, and making a fist closes the gripper. Motion Control: A custom inverse-kinematics solver turns the hand pose into joint angles. A 50 Hz driver moves the arm with safety checks on each joint. Research Ready: Sessions can be recorded and replayed exactly. A ROS 2 interface lets researchers plug in their own trackers, controllers or AI policies. How we built it Mimesis combines stereo computer vision, Kalman filtering, inverse kinematics and real-time servo control, connected through ROS 2. The Hardware: An SO-ARM101 arm with six

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/NUjslLzZx1M?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments We're proud of building a full-stack system that drives a physical robot arm through pick-and-place tasks using only two webcams and natural hand motion. We also cut its latency by more than half and ran hand tracking on NVIDIA, Apple and AMD GPUs. What we learned High-Performance Computing: GPU inference with PyTorch on ROCm, CUDA and Apple MPS, including kernel tuning and FP16 precision. Robotics: Inverse kinematics, Kalman filtering and servo tuning on real hardware. System Integration: Combining computer vision, a cloud server, ROS 2 and a physical robot into one application. What's next Record demonstrations in the LeRobot format and train AI policies that do tasks on their own. Move the cloud server to a region with lower network delay. Special Thanks to AMD We thank AMD for access to the AMD Developer Cloud GPU that powers Mimesis's cloud mode. Built With apple-mpscudafastapigazebomanomediapipenumpyopencvpyside6pythonpytorchrocmros2so-arm101wiloryolo Submitted to Knight Hacks IX Created by I handled most of the ros2 and implementation peytonlynnba

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Network latency was our biggest challenge. The cloud server needs only ~20 ms per frame, but network transit adds about 300 ms per round trip, which we can't reduce from our side. We therefore support two modes: local GPU (~50–60 ms) for the most responsive control, and the AMD cloud server for machines without a GPU. On the hardware side, we had to correct a misfocused camera that caused wrist glitches, joint limits narrower than the robot model's, gravity sag in the shoulder, and a wrist encoder that couldn't rotate past a fixed point. Story evidence excerpt: What it does Apprentice turns one demonstration into a lesson that two very different students can learn: a person and a robot arm. Teach: someone builds a pattern with blocks on a 3x3 board, or records a video of it. Apprentice watches with an overhead camera and turns the build into a step-by-step lesson. Learn: a student rebuilds it while a monitor shows a 3D view of the board. A holographic intro replays the build first. Then, as the student places each block, the camera checks it and gives kind, specific hints like "Close! Move the yellow block one cell to the right", with an arrow on the 3D board. Repeat: press one key and the SO-101 robot arm builds the same lesson. The same camera grades the robot's work with the same checker. When the arm misses, it measures its own error and corrects the next try. Play: Apprentice Buddy is a kids' version on a tablet. Picture Recall shows a p

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/oOZfpdFPcoU?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of One camera grades a human and a robot with the exact same deterministic rules. A cell-by-cell vision model that reads stacks and stays honest: it says "I can't tell" instead of guessing. A robot arm that checks every placement with the camera and corrects itself. A full path from a physical build to a lesson to a game to analytics Our design decisions came from our own data: the stack cap came straight from measured session results. What we learned In physical AI, the hard part is the boring part: lighting, camera angle, line endings, which way the grid is named. Determinism builds trust. Letting language models write hints but never grade made the system predictable enough to put in front of a child. Measure before arguing. Our journal and Databricks tables settled debates that opinions couldn't. Fakes and frozen contracts let four people build one system without blocking each other. What's next for Apprentice Learn from any video: a parent films a build, and Apprentice turns it into a lesson at home. More than blocks: cooking steps, chemistr

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Our cell names were the mirror image of the printed sheet. Only the three diagonal cells meant the same square in the camera code and on the paper and robot side, so the arm and the hints disagreed with the paper. We switched everything to the sheet's convention. The blocks hid the corner tags. Our silicone blocks are 47 mm, so a block in a corner cell always covers part of a tag, and the board kept "disappearing". We lock the board's position once on an empty board and then only check the tags we can still see. Tall towers broke the view. Our camera sits only 432 mm above the table, so the top of a 3-block tower leans a full cell over in the image. In real sessions, the 3-high lesson left 45% of boards unreadable, versus 8% on a flat lesson. We used that data to cap stacks at two blocks. The arm knocked over its own towers and pressed down on blocks as it released them. We raised its travel height above the tallest stack, stopped it pushing on release, and made retries learn the arm's error at each height. Lag stacked up. Four separate waits (the camera's vot

What's next evidence: What's next for Apprentice Learn from any video: a parent films a build, and Apprentice turns it into a lesson at home. More than blocks: cooking steps, chemistry lab setups, physical therapy exercises, anything with steps a camera can check. Adaptive difficulty and parent reports built on the learning data: memory span over time, hints needed, common confusions. A camera mounted higher, for taller stacks and bigger builds. Robots that keep improving with every lesson they repeat. Story evidence excerpt: What it Does Glint is an eye-movement communication prototype for people who can't speak or use their hands. It reads electrooculography (EOG), which is the small voltage changes your eyes produce when they move or blink. A person can pick a phrase or sentence and have it spoken aloud without touching anything. BioAmp EXG Pill sensors on the face feed an Arduino Nano R4. A Python host turns those signals into commands: Glance left, right, up or down to move around a four-slot phase ring. Double blink to select. Long blink to go back. Glasses with a built in OLED screen displaying the four-slot phase ring. The HUD is in constant access for the person, allowing them to make their decisions without needed a laptop or phone to showcase the web application. The web application showcases Glint interface and customization in it's entirety, with access to the four-slot phase ring, different

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/RdWFo5mzaqE?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're Proud of It works. A full loop runs end to end: someone asks a question out loud, it is transcribed on the Jetson, the reply model suggests answers, they appear on the wheel in the glasses, the wearer looks at one, double blinks, and it is spoken aloud. Nothing is ever spoken without that deliberate double blink. EOG. We went from a detector that missed every movement in replay to one where left, right and double-blink selection work in live use. We also built the tooling to get there: every session is recorded, and any decision the detector made can be replayed exactly to find out why. We are honest about the limits: up and down are still unreliable, and this has been tested on one wearer, not on the people it is meant for. Glasses. The glasses show the live wheel, the current status, and a full-screen "DOUBLE BLINK" cue at the exact moment a blink will be accepted, so the wearer does not need a laptop in view. On the website, the whole screen frame changes colour by state (green to choose, blue to return to center, purple to blink, red to retry), becau

Challenges evidence: Challenges We Ran Into Everything. Getting the EOG sensors was an exciting but terrifying experience due to how different they were from the rest of the project. Blinks and glances look alike. Looking up and blinking both show up on the same vertical sensor as a similar pulse. Left and right were dependable; up and down kept getting mistaken for blinks. We ended up redesigning the wheel so the two replies sit on left and right, the directions we could trust. The signal drifts. Our detector matches each eye movement against example movements we recorded. Examples recorded in the morning matched poorly by the evening as the electrodes shifted and dried, so correct glances were being thrown away as "uncertain". A double blink we could not see. Our first double-blink detection failed in live use even though the blinks were clear. Replaying the recording sample by sample showed the detector went briefly "deaf" right after the eyes returned to center, exactly when the blinks happened. We added a second detector that watches for blink pairs continuously. Telling a return from a command. 

What's next evidence: What's Next for Glint Glint is a powerful proof of concept, and we have plenty of ideas and upgrades. We're focused on truly creating the best experience for people who need it most. Full portability: Right now Glint needs a lot of different devices to have it working. We want one small, battery-powered unit you can mount on a wheelchair or a bed and the glasses with EOG capabilities. The Jetson already runs our offline speech and reply models, so the next step is putting the eye-control on it too. A caregiver should be able to set it up from a phone. Optimized networking: We want the whole loop, from eye movement to spoken word, to feel instant. Today our screens ask the server for updates over and over, so we’ll switch to live updates that push the change the moment you look. We’ll also trim the trip your voice takes to the Jetson for speech recognition, and cache or pre-generate the voice for the phrases you use most. Improved hardware: Today it’s DIY glasses, EOG sensors, and a random Jetson Orin Nano. We’d like a wireless headband or glasses frame with more electrodes, which s

Story evidence excerpt: What it does The idea RiskDiff takes a company's two most recent 10-Ks, compares the Risk Factors sections sentence by sentence, and shows you exactly what changed. Type in a ticker RiskDiff pulls both filings straight from the SEC, finds the Risk Factors section, and splits it into individual risks. It then matches each risk to last year's version, even if it was reworded or moved, so wording that changed slightly but means the same thing doesn't show up as a new risk. What changed is always decided by fixed rules in code, so the same two filings give the same result every time. AI only helps with the matching and with short summaries that are clearly labeled. What it shows you A label for every risk factor: new risk, dropped risk, expanded, scaled back, major update, or essentially unchanged. ### [KYU-BOT](https://devpost.com/software/kyu-bot)

Card: KYU-BOT A disaster management system leveraging 3D city physics and multi agent AI to synthesize incoming casualty reports, plan optimal dispatch routes, and score situational awareness against ground truth. 2 1

Tech tags: gemini, openstreetmap, postgresql, react-native. Hardware mentions: quest.

Sponsor opt-ins: [MLH] Best Use of Gemini API, Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, Full Sail: Pathways & Perception, A Serious Games & Simulation Challenge, AMD: Best Use of AMD Technologies, Databricks: Best Use of Databricks, Neon: Best Use of Neon. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does KYU-BOT generates a high fidelity earthquake around Shinjuku, Tokyo using real 3D city data, soil amplification maps, and Japanese seismic engineering formulas. It calculates the exact hidden truth: which builds collapse, which roads are blocked by debris, and where people are trapped or hurt. Then, it streams a chaotic feed of around 120 calls, social media posts, and eye witness texts. These reports include real world noise such as duplicate calls, exaggerated injury counts, wrong locations, and false rumors. Next, a team of specialized AI agents process the incoming report stream: Medical Agent: Filters injury reports into a prioritized triage list showing trapped / critical victims per location. Verification & Assessment Agents: Merge duplicate reports and verify claims against existing evidence. Routing & Resource Agents: Find eligible rescue teams and plot route

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of We are proud of the fact that we were able to create and use a very complex database schema on a new database. Furthermore, we were also able to successfully fuse real 3D building data from PLATEAU, soil amplification models and road networks into one unified map. What we learned We learnt a lot about how to use a Databricks and Neon, and were able to delve into the world of 3D modeling and simulations. We also learnt how to orchestrate multiple AI Agents. We were also able to learn a lot about how to create systems and pipelines to ensure that we have efficient workflows. What's next for KYU-BOT While Japan has an incredible national disaster communication infrastructure, a catastrophic earthquake can still fracture internet connectivity. That's why our natural next step for KYU-BOT is local, edge native deployment. This would allow the entire multi agent pipeline to run entirely offline on local hardware, ingesting radio and mesh network traffic to coordinate life saving rescues even when the grid goes completely dark Built With databri

### [Beacon](https://devpost.com/software/recall-bou9l6)

Card: Beacon Every face has a story to it. Remember it. 0 0

Tech tags: node.js, python. Hardware mentions: quest, camera.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, Full Sail: Pathways & Perception, A Serious Games & Simulation Challenge, Neon: Best Use of Neon. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Beacon turns Meta smart glasses into a wearable visual-memory assistant. The glasses stream camera frames through an Android phone to our backend, where faces are detected and converted into embeddings. Beacon can recognize someone it has seen before, display their name or notes on the glasses, and record where and when they were encountered. Users can name people, add notes, create watch alerts, and ask where somebody was last seen. Beacon also builds a map from the wearer’s movement and can guide them back to a person’s last known location. Full frames and face crops are stored privately so users can review and correct recognition results. Story evidence excerpt: What it does Hold Short is an interactive, AI-assisted airport ground-control simulator built around a digital representation of Newark Liberty International Airport (KEWR). The simulator dynamically introduces aircraft into the airport environment, calculates taxi routes, and tracks their movement across a network of real airport taxiways. Users can observe aircraft moving throughout the airport, intervene in routing decisions, and issue commands to manage traffic. One of Hold Short's central features is its ability to detect potentially dangerous situations, including aircraft separation conflicts and runway incursions. The system analyzes aircraft and vehicle movements to identify hazards and support timely controller intervention. Our project also explores incorporating fatigue detection to monitor controller attentiveness and adjust the urgency of warnings accordingly. Through in

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of One of our proudest accomplishments was transforming real geographic airport data into a functioning, interactive ground-control simulation. Rather than relying on a completely fictional airport layout, we developed a system that represents actual taxiway connections and allows aircraft to navigate through them. We're particularly proud of implementing an integrated backend capable of automatically spawning aircraft, assigning routes, updating their positions, and detecting potential conflicts as the simulation progresses. Seeing aircraft move across the frontend's airport map using routes generated by our own algorithms was an important milestone because it demonstrated that our routing, simulation, and visualization systems could operate together. Another accomplishment was designing Hold Short around meaningful user interaction. Instead of simply displaying aircraft movements, we wanted users to participate in managing airport traffic and understand how their decisions could affect operational safety. This helped transform a technically compl

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into One of our biggest challenges was translating real-world airport infrastructure into a usable simulation environment. Airport taxiways are much more complex than simple paths between points, and we needed to ensure that the graph used for routing matched the airport layout displayed on the frontend. Maintaining consistent geographic identifiers between the backend and visualization was essential to prevent aircraft from appearing in incorrect locations or following invalid routes. Another major challenge involved coordinating multiple moving aircraft while accounting for shared taxiways, runway access, and potential conflicts. It wasn't enough to find the shortest route between two locations; our routing and simulation systems also needed to consider when different aircraft would occupy specific segments of the airport. We also encountered challenges integrating separately developed frontend and backend components. Working across different GitHub branches introduced merge conflicts involving dependencies, configuration files, and API functionality. Resolving

What's next evidence: What's next for Hold Short Looking ahead, we want to expand Hold Short into a more sophisticated and realistic air traffic control training environment. One of our primary goals is to further develop our AI-assisted routing capabilities using historical aircraft movement data, allowing the system to make increasingly informed predictions about taxi times, congestion, and potential conflicts. We also want to expand the simulator's interactive features, including more detailed vehicle controls, runway-incursion challenges, and scoring systems that compare human controller decisions against automated routing. Improving conflict alerts with natural-language explanations, voice notifications, and predicted time-to-conflict information would make the experience more immersive and educational. Other potential improvements include integrating speech recognition for spoken ATC clearances, expanding fatigue detection and controller monitoring, and supporting additional airports and traffic scenarios. Ultimately, we envision Hold Short as more than a hackathon demonstration. We want it to s

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Say What happens "Belt, find my wallet." Finds it, walks you there around obstacles, then hands over to the ring at arm's length. The ring pulses faster as your hand gets closer and gives a solid buzz when you're on it. "Belt, where did I leave my keys?" Answers from memory ("on the table behind you, to your right, about 2 meters away"), then asks "Want me to take you there?" "This is my wallet." Hold it up and the belt learns your wallet, not just any wallet. "Belt, find me a seat." Finds a chair nobody is sitting in and walks you to it. "Belt, take me to the door." Walks you to the nearest door and buzzes when you arrive. "Belt, what's in front of me?" A short spoken description, obstacles first. Walk mode Dodges obstacles from ankle to head height in real time, fully offline. "Stop." Stops everything, any time. How we built it iPhone 17 (eyes + ears) ──WebSocke

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/waQ_jZ7fbVU?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of A complete wearable built in one weekend: custom firmware, code-generated CAD, an iOS app, an edge-AI server and a live judge dashboard. Navigation that keeps working with the Wi-Fi off. The cloud only adds understanding and voice. A ring that solves the "last meter", and a memory that answers "where did I leave it?" What we learned Profile before you optimize. Our biggest bottleneck was a library default we never asked for. Geometry beats guessing. Exact pinhole maths and ARKit's pose made the difference between a belt that buzzes at the floor and one that buzzes at the table. Keep the LLM out of the control loop. Gemini chooses goals; local code owns every motor command. That kept the system safe, fast, and working offline. Haptics need to be unmistakable, not subtle. Small steering corrections felt like noise. Exaggerating dodges into a clear 45° side cue made them readable. Edge AI is a systems problem. Threads, memory, power modes, quotas, serial resets and thermal limits mattered as much as the models did. What's next Testing with blind 

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Frame rate on an 8 GB edge device. Profiling showed the Hugging Face depth pipeline took 110 ms per call, because it quietly upscaled our 320×240 input to 518 px. Calling the model directly in fp16 took 33 ms. YOLO-World spent 215 ms re-encoding text every time we switched between "find the target" and "scan for memory". The fix was one fixed vocabulary, a separate thread for each model, and TensorRT for YOLO11n. "Wallet" kept turning into "phone." Open-vocabulary detectors confuse look-alikes, so we put the look-alikes into the vocabulary on purpose, and require several sightings to agree before trusting a new location. Reach only triggered when you were standing on top of the item. Scale drift in monocular depth made everything look farther away. Per-frame re-scaling and measuring to the item's near surface fixed it. Memory limits. Loading a bigger detector next to depth, tracking and CLIP ran the 8 GB board out of memory. We kept the small YOLO-World, load models one at a time, and guard every optional model with a free-memory check. A motor right behind th

What's next evidence: What's next Testing with blind and low-vision users, a lighter belt with fewer separate parts, stair and curb detection, and running the language model on-device so the whole system works offline. Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments Connected headset driving, live onboard perception, autonomous navigation, and mission evidence in one search-and-rescue prototype. Built a local vision/voice assistant with spoken answers and bilingual survivor phrases on the rover's Jetson. Turned real rover captures into walkable Gaussian-splat rooms with a Mac pipeline based on Dechante's LiDAR-seeded Brush method. Built a room and its face evidence in 114 seconds locally on an M5 Pro from a saved 60-second / 119-photo capture sample. Brush took 112 seconds at 5,000 steps, with 62 seconds of CPU face processing in parallel. A 10-second idle allowance and local-copy stand-in bring that to 124 seconds, excluding real Jetson transfer and browser loading. The sample reuses a longer run's LiDAR seed; it is not a live short-drive end-to-end timing. Added a browser walkthrough that connects face pins to the original boxed camera frames, making the room useful for reviewing what the rover saw. What we learned Give each device the work that fits it: the phone captures depth and pose, the Jetson handles perception and na

Challenges evidence: Challenges We had to make camera streaming, perception, navigation, and motor control share a small computer and an uneven cellular link. Mapping and reconstruction also belong on different schedules: a detailed post-run room should not delay the live camera or control path. A convincing reconstruction needs coherent camera poses and useful views. Parked stretches repeat images; malformed JPEGs can stop Brush; an oversized LiDAR seed can make a short training budget much slower. Reducing splat count for a headset can also introduce visible streaks even when the original training score looks good. We kept measured LiDAR, learned reconstruction, simulation, and replay distinct. A face location is evidence of a sighting, not proof of a unique person, and a model-generated note still needs a responder's judgment. Accomplishments Connected headset driving, live onboard perception, autonomous navigation, and mission evidence in one search-and-rescue prototype. Built a local vision/voice assistant with spoken answers and bilingual survivor phrases on the rover's Jetson. Turned real rov

What's next evidence: What's next Improve room quality within a predictable end-to-end build budget, including transfer, face processing, and publication. Bring the browser's face-evidence interaction into the immersive Quest viewer. Evaluate localization, detection, and operator performance across repeated physical search-training runs, and make multi-room review easier. Sponsor tracks Hugging Face — Best Use of Open Source AI: local vision, speech recognition, and synthesis use Hugging Face-distributed models for a responder's workflow. NVIDIA — Best Use of Jetson Nano: the Orin Nano runs onboard perception, local AI, navigation integration, camera streaming, and motor control. Encompass — Data Capturing Hardware: a rover-mounted LiDAR iPhone turns synchronized real-world observations into encounter records and a 3D room. Full Sail — Pathways & Perception: pathfinding, face detection, timed rescue drill scoring, and room walkthroughs support a search-training scenario. Tech tags: arduino, c++, cuda, javascript, jetson-nano, opencv, python, pytorch, raspberry-pi, three.js. Hardware mentions: Jetson Orin Nano 8 GB, LiDAR iPhone via Record3D, Arduino Uno, L298N, XREAL glasses.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, NVIDIA: Best Use of Jetson Nano, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Permanence gives a robot a persistent 3D memory of objects. The rover scans the table by itself, identifies the objects, and keeps track of where they are as they get hidden or leave the camera's view. Our clearest demonstration is a shell game. Show the robot colored balls, cover them with cups, shuffle the cups, then reveal. Permanence tracks which cup holds each hidden ball and checks its answer against the real reveal. It called the right cup in 7 of 7 recorded games and 3 of 3 live. You can also: Talk to it. "Hey rover, the cup next to the green ball": a 3D vision-language model picks the object from the scan and shows how confident it is. See its memory. A live 3D dashboard and an x-ray display on XREAL glasses show hidden objects through the cups covering them. See what's seen versus remembered, including how long each object has been out of sight. Send it to a re

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/GexOIHp7s1w?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of The full loop on a palm-sized edge board: perception, memory, language, visualization and physical action, with a 3B vision-language model and a real-time tracker together in 8 GB. The reveal. The robot commits to where the hidden ball is, and we lift the cup to check. Nobody has to take our word for it. What we learned Recognizing an object is only the beginning. Useful intelligence needs continuity: what was there, what changed, and what should still be there when you can't see it. Memory is a design constraint, not an afterthought. Deciding what runs when mattered as much as which models we picked. The strongest demo is a claim you can check: show it, hide it, move it, reveal it. What's next for Permanence Prediction: a learned world model that guesses what happened out of view, instead of only remembering what it saw. Bigger hardware: Jetson Thor, so the language model can stay loaded and answer at any time. Better hardware and integration: wheel encoders, and ROS 2 / Isaac ROS. Beyond the tabletop, with clearer uncertainty when memory get

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into 8 GB is small for two models. Loading both crashed the board, and an earlier run left behind can eat the room. We added the handoff, our own quantization, and cleanup of leftover processes. A wobbly camera. With the phone mounted high on the rover, scanning while moving gave blurred frames. Stopping for each shot fixed it. Cheap motors. They stall when started too gently and hum instead of turning, so the rover measures its own speed and adjusts power. When its left and right turned out to be swapped, it detects that and corrects it in software. Disappearing isn't the same as hidden. A missed detection doesn't mean the ball went into a cup. We needed geometry, motion and containment checks, and matching in 3D so identical cups don't swap identities when they cross. Freshness over completeness. An overlay that shows where the world was seconds ago is useless, so every stage takes only the newest frame. Accomplishments that we're proud of The full loop on a palm-sized edge board: perception, memory, language, visualization and physical action, with a 3B vision

What's next evidence: What's next for Permanence Prediction: a learned world model that guesses what happened out of view, instead of only remembering what it saw. Bigger hardware: Jetson Thor, so the language model can stay loaded and answer at any time. Better hardware and integration: wheel encoders, and ROS 2 / Isaac ROS. Beyond the tabletop, with clearer uncertainty when memory gets old. Longer term: assistive robots and glasses that help people find misplaced things. The coughing baby had this figured out in its first year. Now our robot does too. Out of sight should not mean out of memory. Built with: NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano, TensorRT, CUDA, PyTorch, Qwen-3D, YOLOE, Record3D, iPhone LiDAR, Python, Three.js, JavaScript, Web Speech API, XREAL, Arduino, L298N, Raspberry Pi Pico W. Story evidence excerpt: What it does Fireline is a flock of tree-mounted camera units and a live console for the crew that manages them. Each unit runs a smoke detection model on its own NVIDIA Jetson, wakes up only when the weather says fire is likely, and sends a single alert packet of 340 bytes or less over a satellite link. The console puts every unit on a map and sounds the alarm the moment one of them confirms smoke. Smoke detection on the device: Pyronear's open YOLO smoke model runs on the Jetson's GPU. Smoke must appear in at least 4 of the last 6 frames, which filters out clouds, fog and dust. Weather-gated wake-up: a cheap humidity sensor on an ESP32 sets a fire-risk tier. The Jetson scans every 30 minutes when it's humid, every 5 minutes when it's drying out, and continuously in critical fire weather. Satellite-sized alerts: every message fits the 340-byte limit of an Iridium short burst data mess

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Atlas is one everyday tool: a personal AI computer (an NVIDIA Jetson) that connects to your devices and apps, remembers what happens across them, and helps with your day. Plugs into anything. Every device or app is a connector: reads sync into Atlas's memory, and anything that writes to the outside world waits for your tap. Today: a camera, the web (search and Reddit through a real browser), Canvas, your Outlook calendar, GroupMe, the campus site, your watch's health data, and voice on your phone. Smart lights, a gate or a car plug in the same way. Remembers. Everything it hears about becomes events in an append-only log: its memory. The camera is one source: detection, tracking and zones run on the Jetson, and faces are blurred before anything is stored. Answers in plain English, with evidence. "What happened at our table overnight?" "Where did I leave my keys?" "What's d

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/Qrngo1jIO6E?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of Everything at runtime happens on the Jetson: one box for memory, answers and actions across every connected device; the GPUs are only where Atlas learns. Every answer cites its evidence, and every outside action needs a tap. From the box's own log on Saturday: ~10,700 frames seen, 9,738 objects and people observed, 19 episodes remembered, 55 cited answers, 725 calendar/Canvas/chat items read. YOLO11n TensorRT FP16 at 41 FPS; a 30-minute soak at 15 fps with 0 errors; Qwen3.5-4B at 13–14 tok/s on the Orin Nano. What we learned Pre-fetching beats tool loops for small models. An append-only event log makes a system safe to change while it runs. Consent is a product feature: approval cards made the agent feel trustworthy, not risky. What's next for Atlas More connectors: smart lights, a garage gate, the car, so Atlas can see more of your life and (with a tap) act on it. More learned skills (food, drink and recyclable recognition). Replays of real footage: frame interpolation on AMD turns approved, blurred keyframes into a short video. More campus 

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Making a 4B model reliable. Multi-step tool calling is shaky on a small quantized model, so the most common questions skip the tool loop. We wrote 48 ways a judge might ask our 8 demo questions: 35/42 at first, 48/48 after the fixes, answers in 0.6s. Losing our GPUs mid-hackathon. The hub with our two Radeon AI PRO R9700s went down on Saturday, so we moved the lab to two Strix Halo pods and rebuilt vLLM for a new chip. Fine-tuning on AMD. A 27B model is ~54 GB in bf16, too big to train on one device, which is why we went teacher-student. Bot checks. Headless browsers get CAPTCHAs; a visible, persistent profile gets through for the sites a person would visit anyway. Replay safety. Rebuilding state from the log must never resend a message or repeat an approved action, so handlers opt in to replay explicitly. Accomplishments that we're proud of Everything at runtime happens on the Jetson: one box for memory, answers and actions across every connected device; the GPUs are only where Atlas learns. Every answer cites its evidence, and every outside action needs a 

What's next evidence: What's next for Atlas More connectors: smart lights, a garage gate, the car, so Atlas can see more of your life and (with a tap) act on it. More learned skills (food, drink and recyclable recognition). Replays of real footage: frame interpolation on AMD turns approved, blurred keyframes into a short video. More campus connectors and place packs, so Day Run works on any campus. Story evidence excerpt: What it does Maps as it drives: a 2D lidar builds a floor plan live with SLAM, and the robot plans its own survey points on that map. Captures 360° "bubbles": at each survey point the robot spins in place, stopping every 30° to take 12 photos, each tagged with its heading. Finds and names plants: an open-vocabulary detector finds plant-like objects, and a biology model names the species and flags it as invasive or native. Pins each plant on the map: the plant's position in the image plus the lidar range in that direction places it on the floor plan, with a cropped photo saved for each pin. VR walkthrough: a Meta Quest app shows a tabletop miniature of the map with the robot, bubbles, and pins. Pick a bubble and you are standing inside it, with the plants marked around you. Before and after: survey once, remove the invasives, survey again, and the app shows which plants are gone and whic

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/2cIQGjinX3I?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of A full chain from camera frame to named, mapped plant, built only from open-source models and running offline Detection at camera speed (15 frames per second) on an 8 GB Jetson, alongside the species model A VR view where you can stand inside a spot the robot photographed and see what it found there A before/after comparison that turns two surveys into one number Two computers, a headset, and a microcontroller sharing one map over a phone hotspot Four people building in parallel against a shared contract, with a fallback for every fragile part What we learned How to split a robot's work across a Pi, a Jetson, and a microcontroller with ROS 2 Chaining a general detector with a specialist classifier, and why a "none of the above" option matters How much sensor fusion depends on timestamps, clocks, and measured offsets Building a Unity app against a robot that doesn't exist yet Thermal and power limits decide where code runs as much as software does What's next for Understory An outdoor chassis that can handle real understory terrain A real befor

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Heat: running both models on the Pi 5 pushed it to 83 °C in five minutes and throttled the CPU, so the AI moved to the Jetson. Speed: OWL-ViT takes about 3 seconds per frame on the Pi's CPU, so the detector drops frames and the species step matches each box to its original frame by timestamp. Lookalike plants: in photos, cogongrass looks like grass and wild coffee looks like leaves. Both scored so low that we dropped the detector threshold to 0.05 and relied on BioCLIP to reject the junk. False positives: a ceiling decoration was confidently named cogongrass until we added the "not a plant" list. Faces and decorations: the first live pins were a face named Brazilian pepper and an artificial flower garland named air potato. Adding other species by scientific name fixed it. Pi power and clock: the Pi rebooted several times and has no clock battery; it once came back almost six hours slow, which breaks ROS transforms between machines. USB power: on a power bank the Pi 5 limits its USB ports, so the lidar kept dropping out with over-current errors until we raised 

What's next evidence: What's next for Understory An outdoor chassis that can handle real understory terrain A real before-and-after survey of a habitat, to replace our staged data A larger species list, including animals Soil moisture, light, and air quality readings shown as heat maps on the map Before/after reports and similar-plant search in the dashboard for land managers Full 3D Gaussian splat reconstructions of each survey site GPS so surveys line up across weeks and seasons Built With adafruitandroidarduinoc#dockeresp32jetsonjetson-nanolidarmetaquestnav2opencvopenxrpythonpytorchraspberry-piros2slamunityvr Submitted to Knight Hacks IX Created by kevin li Mizbah Ateeq Anthony Alvaro Canseco-Martinez Hi, there! Story evidence excerpt: What it does Waymaker is a backpack wearable for blind and low-vision people that runs fully offline on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano. Feel obstacles: two ultrasonic sensors and a chest camera drive vibration motors under the left and right backpack straps. Pulses speed up as obstacles get closer, and turn steady when it's time to stop. Understand the street: an on-device YOLO11n model tracks people, bikes, poles, and other obstacles, and predicts whether each one is actually on a collision course. A second model reads pedestrian crossing signals and announces when they change. Ask about the view: hold the button on the strap, ask "What does that sign say?", and Waymaker answers through your AirPods using a local vision-language model. Safety first: crossing-signal alerts interrupt the assistant mid-sentence, and the motors never wait on the AI. If the camera fails, the ultrasonic sensors

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of A complete pipeline, from sensors to vibration to spoken answers, that works with zero internet. Warnings that can tell the difference between someone walking toward you and someone walking past. A custom 3D-printed case for the Jetson Orin Nano. Training our own classification model on data we labeled by hand. What we learned Safety alerts must be instant while answers can take a few seconds. Two cheap sensors that cover each other's blind spots can beat one expensive one. On edge devices, memory runs out before compute does. Test the hardware first. Plan and label your wiring before building. What's next for Waymaker Testing with blind users and orientation and mobility instructors. A lighter enclosure and longer battery life. Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Memory: the CPU and GPU share 8 GB, so every model competes for space. False alarms: early versions buzzed at people just walking past. A dead board: our first Jetson wouldn't boot, and it took us a while to realize the board was broken. New to hardware: it was our first time with circuits, so we learned by trial and error. Wiring: at 11 p.m. Saturday we realized we needed Grove cables. We asked other teams for spares, then made our own with tape. Loose wires: our chained jumper wires kept disconnecting and were hard to track. Scarce data: we couldn't find enough labeled data, so we annotated images by hand. ### [Tattle](https://devpost.com/software/tattle)

Card: Tattle Your phone is tattling on you. Tattle shows every company your apps secretly contact, live, and explains it in plain English. It sees hostnames only and stores nothing. 2 0

Tech tags: css, html, javascript, python, shell. Hardware mentions: Jetson, Raspberry Pi.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, AMD: Best Use of AMD Technologies. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Tattle is a laptop that acts as a Wi-Fi hotspot. A phone joins it, with nothing installed on the phone. Before an app can connect to a server it has to ask "where is this server?" (a DNS lookup), and on our Wi-Fi we are the one it asks. So we see the name of every server the phone looks up, and never what is sent afterwards. The screen shows the phone in the middle and one bubble per company around it, coloured and tagged by what the company does: advertising, analytics, social, fingerprinting, content delivery, or the app's own servers. A click on a bubble opens a panel that says who the company is and lists every server name the phone asked for. One more click blocks the company: we answer "that name does not exist", the app cannot reach it, and a counter shows the app trying again and again while it keeps working. Who it helps Anyone who wants to know what an app real
