Story evidence excerpt: What it does HearthWatch connects local camera observations and care check-ins to a nurse response workflow: Possible Fall Detection: Our own posture model, trained by us (using timed labels from Hugging Face's OmniFall) and running on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano, labels everyone in view as upright, sitting, or on the floor. Prolonged floor tagging notifies a nurse to check in. Gesture Help Requests: Recognize a held fist as a request for assistance, with a manual help button as another option. Nurse Subscriptions: Let nurses select rooms and alert categories, receive updates, and accept responsibility for a response. Appointment Reminders: Notify the care team when a patient's arrival is not confirmed within 15 minutes of a scheduled appointment. Camera Health: Flag disconnected sources, stale observations, and missing visibility. Simulation and Replay: Explore resident activities and stage

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/iJhuGaqfXCg?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments Training our own posture model that, on a locked test of 10,333 frames from pemacro F1 of 97.0% (weighted 97.5%): Floor: precision 97.9%, recall 96.1%, specificity 99.3% (a 0.67% false positive rate) Upright: precision 98.4%, recall 98.6% Sitting: precision 95.5%, recall 95.7% On our own camera it catches 96% of floor frames. Running it live on a 2 GB Jetson Nano for every person in view, with the panel warning when anyone is on the floor. Connecting observations to a complete alert -> nurse acceptance -> resolution workflow Bringing the live Jetson feed into the 3D simulation, so real and simulated residents share one room. Supporting fist help requests even when the posture model cannot detect a body Adding persistent appointment reminders and a clearly labelled 15-second demonstration mode. Evaluating the real monitor without giving it access to simulator ground truth What we learned Temporal Reasoning: Useful alerts depends on evidence over time. Honest Metrics: Splitting by person and locking a test set matters. Validation that shares rooms and cameras with 

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Camera Placement: Height-based floor rules depended heavily on camera position. Our trained model judges body shape instead of position in the frame, so it only needs the whole body in view. Incomplete Body Detection: Lying bodies were difficult for poseNet to capture. Rotated inference passes improved keypoint coverage, and we extended them to everyone in view, not just the tracked person. Unstable Predictions: Brief gestures, low-confidence classifications, and missing frames could interrupt detection. We added hold times, confidence thresholds, release conditions, and cooldowns. Live and Simulated Clocks: Accelerated playback needed to preserve posture timing while camera health reflected actual arrival times. We separated the clocks and isolated replay runs with unique identifiers. Identity Without Overclaiming: Face detection establishes presence, not identity. Appointment check-in uses an explicit token. Recognition (YuNet + SFace) is a separate, opt-in demo with fictional profiles. Accomplishments Training our own posture model that, on a locked test 

What's next evidence: What's next for HearthWatch Motion-Aware Model: A model that reads several frames at once, to tell falling to handle close-ups and partial views. Broader Testing: Evaluate more camera positions, lighting conditions, mobility aids, and real older adults rather than young actors staging falls. Appointment Interface: Add a calendar and accessible resident check-in control Resident Feedback: Make it clearer when a help request is received and a nurse is responding. Access and Retention: Add authentication, protected room access, and configuraing real resident records. Expanded Evaluation: Report detection delays, missed events, and false alerts across a larger set of scenarios. ### [VIGIL](https://devpost.com/software/vigil-1fx8gp)

Card: VIGIL Low-cost thermal person detection and geolocation for search-and-rescue (SAR) drones. 7 0

Tech tags: c++, python, react, typescript. Hardware mentions: Custom STM32 flight board, FLIR Lepton 3.5, XIAO ESP32-S3, ExpressLRS radio, GPS.

Sponsor opt-ins: Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, Adobe: Prettiest Hack. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does VIGIL is a low cost thermal search-and-rescue drone prototype designed to help operators locate people when visibility is limited. The drone cost under $500 to build, which is nearly 1/10th the price of the nearest competitor. It combines a thermal camera, person-detection AI, and a ground-station dashboard. Operators can watch live thermal footage with tracked person detection, review each suspected person using the exact image that triggered the detection, and see estimated locations when enough telemetry is available. Operators can also add or remove person markers manually and create search routes. Locally saved maps and terrain elevation data allow prepared mission areas to remain available without internet access. How we built it Lepton 3.5 → ESP32-S3 (Wi-Fi) → ground computer (YOLO26 detection + tracking) → Socket.IO → web dashboard Flight controller (INAV) → Expr

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/0jwwaFb0ppM?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of We're proud of connecting custom hardware, thermal AI, and a practical operator interface. Our documented camera bench testing achieved approximately 8.8 unique thermal frames per second at native resolution. We developed a custom INAV target and achieved a stable hover, with roll and pitch held within about 1 degree. The drone streams attitude, barometric altitude, GPS position, and a calibrated compass heading to our dashboard over ExpressLRS. On live Lepton footage, our blur-trained model detected the person in 94% of frames of an indoor test session, at an average confidence of 0.78. Every live session is recorded, so we can replay it offline and tune detection without the camera attached. The dashboard provides a human-review workflow that preserves the matching thermal image and bounding box for each detected person. Manual markers let operators supplement the model's observations. We also built automated tests for critical interfaces, and offline map and terrain support. What we learned We learned that reliable detection depends heavi

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Low-resolution thermal detection was a major challenge. Distant people can occupy only a few pixels, and the Lepton's slow sensor smears anyone who moves. Models trained on clean datasets lost confidence as soon as someone walked, so we fine-tuned on synthetically blurred, noisy frames. Hot objects were the next problem: a shorted circuit board was once detected as a person, which led to the temperature check. Overlapping boxes followed, with one person detected as separate head and body boxes; we fixed that with custom merging rules. Reliable camera capture required careful handling of packet synchronization, memory buffers, and network delays. We patched the camera library and separated frame acquisition from streaming. Getting a 7 inch drone to hover stably. Our first takeoff flipped instantly. This added a 12 hour delay to our progress right in the middle of the event. We traced it to a gyroscope high-pass filter in INAV's driver for our IMU that removed the bias a second time after calibration, leaving a constant false roll. Later flights were better, b

What's next evidence: What's next for VIGIL Our next priority is completing hardware bring-up and controlled flight validation, progressing from stable manual flight to altitude hold, GPS navigation, and waypoint missions. We need to calibrate camera mounting, heading, and image-to-telemetry latency, then measure location error against known ground targets. For the AI, our live testing so far has been at close range. Next we want in-flight Lepton footage across different altitudes, backgrounds, and motion conditions. We'll measure missed detections, false-alert episodes, and detection delay alongside conventional accuracy metrics. We also want systematic area-coverage routes, persistent review records, easier offline mission preparation, and feedback from search-and-rescue operators to guide the next version. Card: Market Maps Lower the physical and mental cost of your grocery trip! A simulation demo that takes your shopping list and calculates the shortest route to every item and the checkout. 0 0

Story evidence excerpt: Inspiration An average able-bodied person's grocery trip can take up to 45 minutes. For shoppers with disabilities, chronic pain, or limited stamina, walking for that long takes a mental and physical toll. Most people wander a grocery store, double back for forgotten items, and cross the store more than once. I wanted a tool that takes a grocery list and gives the shortest possible walk through the store, with clear guidance the whole way. Core loop Type your list. Enter items in plain words. The app understands typos ("bannana"), plurals, and common names ("oj" for juice, "tp" for toilet paper), and tells you when the store doesn't carry something. Get the best route. ### [Swarm](https://devpost.com/software/swarm-oqg6l4)

Card: Swarm Cheap attack drones are now one of the biggest threats to bases and infrastructure. Operators practice against scripted enemies. We built a trainer where the enemy learned to attack 4 0

Tech tags: css3, html5, javascript, python, pytorch, three.js. Hardware mentions: radar.

Sponsor opt-ins: Full Sail: Pathways & Perception, A Serious Games & Simulation Challenge, AMD: Best Use of AMD Technologies. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does SWARM is a 3D training simulation with three phases: Defend. Spend a fixed budget on radars (line-of-sight detection, blocked by terrain), jammers (scramble drone navigation) and interceptor pads. Hover with the radar tool to see exactly what it would cover from that spot. Attack. Choose an enemy: a classic Scripted A* planner, or a swarm trained with reinforcement learning at three difficulties (Recruit, Regular, Veteran). SWARM simulates 64 attacks against your defense and replays a representative one on a sand-table map. Review. An after-action report shows your breach rate across all 64 attacks, the corridors the drones used, an automatic blind-spot diagnosis with a suggested fix, the A* planner's view of your defense, and how you compare to an AI-optimized layout. What sets it apart from other defense simulations: Tactics the swarm found. We never programmed tactics

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://player.vimeo.com/video/1234778075?byline=0&portrait=0&title=0#t=

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of A learned enemy that beats a classic pathfinder on defenses it has never seen, trained from scratch in minutes. Tactics nobody programmed, detected and named automatically. A neural network view that shows the real computation behind each move, not an illustration. Being honest about the numbers: held-out benchmarks, a stall rate on every simulation, and a lower score we chose over a better-looking broken one. A polished, end-to-end simulation that runs offline, with unit tests and an automated UI test across both theaters. What we learned RL agents will exploit any gap in the reward, and an arms race will find the exploit before you do. Writing the simulation as batched tensor operations turns the simulation itself into a training environment for free. Profile on the hardware you actually train on: what's fast on one GPU stack can be 60× slower on another. Pathfinding and learning complement each other. Give the agent a pathfinding field as a sense and it starts at A* level, then learns what A* can't: timing, spreading out and dodging. What's 

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into The swarm that refused to attack. Our first Veteran posted the best numbers, but against the strongest defenses it never attacked at all: it loitered outside radar range until time ran out. Two causes: timing out cost the same as being shot down, and the blue-team search threw away every defense the swarm couldn't beat, including the ones where it hid. Then the AI-layout search found that habit and exploited it. We made timing out 3× as costly, kept the stall-inducing defenses in training, and now reject any checkpoint that stalls on more than 10% of drones. That cost us a few points of headline breach rate, and we report the honest number. ROCm performance. Small convolutions were 30–60× slower than the equivalent matrix multiplies, and fp32 matmuls were about 40× slower than bf16. Switching to a bf16 MLP made training 13× faster. An arms race that stalled. The blue team first kept only unbeatable defenses, which give no learning signal. The fix was to keep the frontier: defenses the swarm beats about 20% of the time. Reward hacking. A penalty for being detec

What's next evidence: What's next Let the player's own defenses feed the blue-team pool, so the swarm keeps adapting to how people actually defend. Heterogeneous swarms (decoys, fast scouts, heavy strikers) and moving or pop-up air defenses. Real elevation data (DEM tiles) so planners can train on terrain they actually defend. To shoot, you grab the string with your right hand and squeeze your fist: the MyoWare 2.0 sensor on your forearm turns that squeeze into draw strength, and the arrow flies when you let go. A short calibration at the start (relax, then squeeze as hard as you can) maps your own range onto the bow, so a strong player and a weak player both get a full draw. A vest with three vibration motors (left, right, back) tells you where a hit came from. The game works out the attacker's direction relative to your head: a bite from the left buzzes the left motor, from the right the right one, and a bite straight to the face fires all three at once. Workspaces are able to be saved and loaded as .json files on the device. Hand Gestures Pinch to grab: pinch an object with your thumb and pointer finger to pick it up and move it. Thumb + index will snap the object to the grid or flush with other objects. Thumb + middle will allow you to move the object freely. Push and pull for depth: move your hand closer to the camera to move an object deeper into the space and pull it away from the camera to bring it closer. Two-hand scale: pinch both hands and bring them apart or together to resize. Two-hand rotate: twist both hands like a steering wheel to turn an object, or move both hands up or down to tilt it. Move the camera: pinch empty space and move to rotate the view. Push or pull to zoom in or out. Pr

### [CrowdClear](https://devpost.com/software/crowdclear)

Card: CrowdClear A Web & Immersive VR emergency evacuation training simulation: spot crowd jams, open exits, unblock obstacles, redirect the flow, and calm evacuators. 1 0

Tech tags: css3, express.js, html5, javascript, node.js, postgresql, three.js. Hardware mentions: Meta Quest 3, Quest 3, camera, microphone.

Sponsor opt-ins: [MLH] Best Use of ElevenLabs, [MLH] Best Use of Presage, [MLH] Best Domain Name from GoDaddy Registry, Full Sail: Pathways & Perception, A Serious Games & Simulation Challenge, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, Neon: Best Use of Neon, Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does CrowdClear is an evacuation training game that runs in a laptop browser or in VR through WebXR. You manage a stadium lobby with 60 people and three exits during a 60-second drill. Guided practice first. Two untimed tasks teach you to open an exit and send people to it. Each step advances only after you actually do it. A drill that escalates. Two or three blockages hit during the drill. The first lands 10 to 20 seconds in, and each one strikes the exit most people are heading for at that moment. Blocked exits stay blocked until you deal with them: clearing takes four seconds, one exit at a time, and reopening the door is a separate step. Sending people to another exit has a five-second cooldown, so timing matters. Panic you have to manage. At each incident, about one in five guests panics. They wander, ignore redirects and won't queue until someone calms them. On a laptop,

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/pNp_bZqdGGw?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of A best-run replay that shows, in the same scene, how two runs played out differently. Runs that rebuild exactly from a seed and a command list, checked by the server before saving. ElevenLabs audio wired to game events, generated ahead of time with no key in the browser. Presage webcam vitals turned into a calm score the trainee can try to beat. One set of door controls shared by mouse and VR controllers. Game balance backed by strategy sweeps over every scenario. What we learned Build determinism in from the start. It gave us replay, server-side checks and testable balance, and every later feature had to respect it. Measure strategies before tuning by feel. The sweeps showed that a strong strategy without calming tops out at 58 of 60 people. Adding calming gets everyone out. Pre-generating ElevenLabs audio suits a timed drill: no delay when an incident hits, no key in the browser, and one source file for every spoken line. Presage gave us a second outcome besides people out: how calm the person in charge stayed while making those calls. The rev

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Keeping replays exact as the game grew. Every new mechanic (timed clearing, multiple blockages, panic, calming) had to stay deterministic or replays would drift. Input at a tick is now applied before that tick's incident, calming goes through recorded commands, and even the Reassure button chooses guests with a seeded shuffle. Testing caught replayed redirects losing their target exit. When the rules change, we bump the simulation version (now 9), so older saved runs say they can't be replayed instead of replaying wrong. Making decisions matter. Early versions cleared themselves without help, and spamming redirects beat thinking. Blocking the busiest exit, a redirect cooldown, slower crowded lines, repeat blockages and panicking guests turned it into a real tradeoff. In the sweeps above, only the bot that spread the crowd early, cleared right away and calmed guests got everyone out. Calming by voice. Our first microphone version calmed guests whenever it heard moderately loud sound held for a fraction of a second. We switched to the browser's speech recognit

What's next evidence: What's next for CrowdClear CrowdClear is a prototype with a deliberately simple crowd model. Its results are simulation outputs, not predictions of real evacuations. Next, we want to: Test pointing accuracy, panel placement, comfort and performance on a physical Quest. Run Presage on hardware it supports natively, and find a way to measure trainees in VR, where the headset hides the face. Replace the single "calm" keyword with real conversations with panicking guests. Add navigable obstacles and more floor plans with proper pathfinding. Add people with mobility needs and coordination between several staff members. Give trainers tools to assign drills and track progress. Compare the crowd model with published evacuation research and get feedback from venue staff. It automates the part of triage a person currently has to do on foot: finding the patient, getting to them, and taking a reading. In autonomous mode it sweeps the room looking for a patient — not just any person, but specifically someone in a medical gown, identified by combining YOLO person detection with a calibrated color signature. A person alone isn't enough and the gown color alone isn't enough; both have to pass before it commits. Once it has a lock, it drives toward them, steering around obstacles with lidar and slowing as it closes, then holds position on arrival. From there it switches from navigating to monitoring. ### [Pulse](https://devpost.com/software/pulse-cpnfut)

Card: Pulse We made a radar that can be used to help find people that are trapped due to natural disasters or other crises, it uses frequencies to find people through walls, and can read also their heart rate. 3 0

Tech tags: css, html, javascript, linux, python, websockets. Hardware mentions: Raspberry Pi 4 2 GB, 7-inch Hosyond touchscreen, HLK-LD2450, DFRobot C1001, USB power bank.

Sponsor opt-ins: Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, OneEthos: Innovation for Good. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Pulse combines two radar sensors to give responders a clearer picture of who may be nearby. The 24 GHz HLK-LD2450 tracks up to three moving targets and plots their positions relative to the device as dots on a radar screen. When someone is within the 60 GHz DFRobot C1001’s sensing range, it can provide breathing and heart rate readings. An operator can tap a dot to view available readings and on-screen guidance for aiming the sensor toward that person. Together, the location and vital sign information can help responders save lives in emergency situations. How we built it We built Pulse from the hardware up in 36 hours, including its sensor drivers, fusion engine, and radar interface. Hardware: A Raspberry Pi 4 with 2 GB of memory runs the system and connects to a 7 inch Hosyond DSI capacitive touchscreen with an 800 × 480 resolution. The HLK LD2450 tracks targets using

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/131dlpYc1ZU?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we’re proud of • Built a handheld prototype with a Raspberry Pi, touchscreen, and two radar sensors in 36 hours. • Connected the Hosyond display to the Pi, configured it to launch Pulse full screen, and used SSH to make changes during development. • Displayed up to three HLK LD2450 targets as dots, using the sensor’s X and Y position readings. • Built a touch interface for selecting targets and viewing available readings. • Wrote sensor parsers and a fusion engine, then developed a simulator and ran 187 automated tests through the fusion code. • Soldered connections to the LD2450 despite its small, closely spaced pins. What we learned Radar fusion takes more than combining data. The LD2450 gives us target positions, while the C1001 provides close range vital readings without a target location. Each sensor has different strengths and limits, so the software has to be careful about when it associates a reading with a dot. Serial communication needs careful setup. Getting two sensors working on separate UARTs meant configuring the Pi’s hardware serial ports

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into We changed our idea late in the process, which left us searching for parts that could arrive in time. Overnight shipping helped us get started, but our limited options meant making compromises in the hardware we could use. The LD2450’s small, closely spaced pins did not match the jumper wires available to us. We had to cut, strip, and solder wires directly to the sensor. Keeping the joints separate was difficult, and we had to be careful to avoid shorting neighboring pins. Neither sensor came with a ready to use Raspberry Pi driver for our setup. We built Python parsers from the sensor documentation and the available DFRobot Arduino library, then worked through serial communication issues on the Pi. Our first readings also made the displayed dots jump around instead of following people smoothly. We traced this to sensor noise and adjusted the movement threshold so the interface responded to more significant movement. That made the display steadier, while also making it less responsive to small movements. Accomplishments that we’re proud of • Built a handh

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Echo turns a forgotten child into a siren within seconds and a phone call within minutes, and it keeps working with no internet. Detects. When the ignition turns off, the box watches the car seat with an ultrasonic sensor and reads cabin temperature every 2 seconds. Predicts. It fits a line through the last 2 minutes of readings and computes minutes until the cabin reaches 104°F. That number comes from real sensor data, never from AI. Alarms. Alert levels climb from Armed to Occupant to Warning (95°F, or 10 minutes to danger) to Emergency (104°F, or 5 minutes). An RGB LED changes color and a siren sweeps. Only a person pressing the button on the box, or turning the car back on, can stop it. Dispatches. At Emergency, plain code starts a backup call ladder: call Contact #1, call again at 2 minutes, call Contact #2 at 3 minutes, alert everyone at 5. An AI voice agent rides on

### [Jolteon](https://devpost.com/software/jolteon)

Card: Jolteon Every data center runs hot. We made that heat pay back. 12 1

Tech tags: c++, javascript, python, react, sql. Hardware mentions: Four SP1848 TEGs, staged hot/cold source, NiMH battery, ESP32, INA219 and DS18B20 sensors.

Sponsor opt-ins: [MLH] Best Domain Name from GoDaddy Registry, Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware, OneEthos: Innovation for Good, Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Jolteon captures waste heat, turns it into stored electricity, and shows you exactly how much it has collected, live. Step What happens 🔥 Heat A hot plate stands in for a server and heats one side of the generators ❄️ Cold An ice bath keeps the other side cold ⚡ Generate Four thermoelectric generators turn that temperature difference into electricity 🔋 Store The current flows straight into a battery pack, no charging chip needed 📈 Track Every reading streams to a public dashboard anyone can open on their phone We picked the battery so its own voltage keeps the generators near their most efficient point. The physics does the regulating for us, which keeps the circuit simple enough to explain in one sentence. How we built it We agreed on one shared JSON format first, so the hardware, firmware, bridge and dashboard could all be built at the same time without stepping

Demo linked (not independently replayed in this audit): https://www.youtube.com/embed/8vnv30qKB9w?enablejsapi=1&hl=en_US&rel=0&start=&version=3&wmode=transparent

Accomplishments evidence: Accomplishments that we're proud of We tried hard to break our own pipeline, and it held up: Test Result Simulated network outages 0 readings lost, 0 duplicates Bridge killed 20 times mid-stream 0 missing, 0 duplicates 1,000 corrupted lines All rejected, 0 crashes Sensor reading to visible in the API ~200 ms Chart data with downsampling 83% smaller Over 3 hours, Jolteon collected 1.2 Wh from heat that would have been thrown away. Every one of those watt-hours is a real measurement, and anyone can scan our QR code and watch the number tick up on their own phone. What we learned Heat is easy to make and hard to move. We assumed "hot laptop" meant "lots of usable heat." It doesn't. Thermal contact, a few degrees here and there, and even which side of a module faces up mattered far more than we expected. Plan for things to break. Saving locally first felt like overkill at the start. Then the Wi-Fi died, the ESP32 kept rebooting, and the cable got bumped, and not one of those cost us a reading thanks to our safety-net data base. Agree on the data before writing code. Settling 

Challenges evidence: Challenges we ran into Our first database couldn't handle the venue Wi-Fi. We started on a different cloud database, but the connection kept failing on the hackathon's network, and a run that lasts all night can't depend on that. Around 5 a.m. we moved everything to Supabase, which talks over plain HTTPS and went through on the first try. The ESP32 resets every time you open its port. Every time the bridge connected, the board rebooted, its packet counter started back at 1, and the database quietly threw away new readings as duplicates. Nothing crashed, the data just vanished. We fixed it by saving the counter to flash and jumping it ahead on every boot, so a number can never be reused. The laptop wasn't hot enough. This was the big one. A laptop's underside is designed to stay cool, and after the heat passes through a thermal pad and two aluminum plates, our generators barely saw a temperature difference, not enough to push a single milliamp into the battery. [We switched to a hot water loop, which turned out to be closer to how modern liquid-cooled data centers actually carry he

What's next evidence: What's next for Jolteon The biggest step is moving onto real heat. Server racks and liquid-cooling loops run hotter and steadier than anything we could set up in a weekend, and that's where these generators start to make sense. After that: a proper energy-harvesting chip that works at smaller temperature differences, an estimate of what a full rack or data center could recover, and cost and carbon numbers on the dashboard so the energy we capture means something to the people who would pay for it. Tech tags: python, pytorch. Hardware mentions: Digilent Arty A7-100T FPGA, USB-UART cable.

Sponsor opt-ins: AMD: Best Use of AMD Technologies. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does A laptop sends four word tokens over the USB-UART cable of a Digilent Arty A7-100T. The FPGA runs the complete neural network in hardware and sends back one byte, the predicted next word. The laptop repeats this to turn a typed prompt into a short plant-care sentence. The network is a small feedforward model over a 64-word vocabulary: $$x = [E_{t_0};\,E_{t_1};\,E_{t_2};\,E_{t_3}]$$ $$h = \mathrm{ReLU}(W_1 x + b_1), \qquad z = W_2 h + b_2, \qquad t_{\text{next}} = \arg\max_i z_i$$ 4-word context, 64 x 8 embedding table, a 32-to-32 layer, a 32-to-64 layer, 3,680 parameters INT8 weights and activations with INT32 accumulators Under a thousand clock cycles per predicted word How we built it Model (Python). We trained the model in PyTorch on a small plant-monitoring corpus, quantized it to integers, and wrote an integer-only Python reference that the hardware must match ex

Press and hold anywhere and say "find the chair", "find the red backpack" or "find the exit sign". It knows 79 everyday objects, matches descriptions, and searches for anything else by name. The gyro remembers where the target is, so if you turn away, you hear "Chair at 2 o'clock", with pulses that get stronger as you face it again. It watches the way. ### [KinematiX](https://devpost.com/software/audit-the-llc)

Story evidence excerpt: What it does The user will take a photo from their phone of the board The photo is sent to OpenCV to find most if not all pins on the board, and determine which cartesian coordinates they are at from the perspective of the robot The user will select which pins they want to probe The robot arm will move the probe to the calculated cartesian point Osciloscope will sample the data off of the pin for X amount of time and send the raw readings to our computer The raw data will get sent to the backend, get cleaned and then sent to a detection algorithm to determine what protcol (UART, PWM etc) and data was being sent over the pin A report is generated that the researcher can view How we built it Hardware Side: We used 2 stepper motors to provide angular movement from 2 centers for X and Y movement, and an additional SG90 motor for Z movement so the probe can touch the contacts on the board.

### [Tilted](https://devpost.com/software/tilted-01dbpv)

Card: Tilted Tilted is a cooperative physics game where players tilt their phones to control one shared board. Team up, coordinate, and don’t get tilted. 0 0

Tech tags: css3, html5, javascript, node.js, three.js, typescript, websockets. Hardware mentions: quest.

Sponsor opt-ins: None. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Tilted is a cooperative multiplayer physics game where players use the physical tilt of their phones to control one shared board. Each phone sends a 2D motion vector representing the player’s tilt. The host combines the players’ inputs into a single team vector, and that resulting direction tilts the game board. The marble then moves naturally through the physics simulation. The core idea is: $$[ \mathbf{T} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{v}_i ]$$ where each $$(\mathbf{v}_i)$$ is one player’s input vector and $$(\mathbf{T})$$ is the team’s combined input. When players coordinate, the marble moves strongly in the intended direction. When they fight each other, the board levels out and the marble slows down. How we built it We built Tilted as a browser-based multiplayer experience so players can join from their phones without installing an app. The project consists

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Quantum Privacy Firewall (QPF) is a privacy-aware quantum compilation and auditing framework. It analyzes structural information leaked by transpiled circuits and generates functionally equivalent alternatives through gate transformations, layout diversification, and transpiler randomization. It verifies correctness, measures hardware overhead, and evaluates privacy improvements against adaptive attackers. How we built it We used IBM Qiskit and FakeNighthawk to implement a benchmark covering seven algorithm classes, 47 circuit generators, and 24/48-qubit workloads. Ten normalized structural features are analyzed using Random Forest classifiers. Our defense pipeline uses MQT QCEC and other equivalence checks alongside resource-budget filtering. A FastAPI backend and React frontend provide interactive circuit analysis and visualization. Challenges we ran into Our biggest

### [Fobo](https://devpost.com/software/fobo-fear-of-burning-out)

Card: Fobo We all know FOMO, but what about the fear of burning out? Fobo helps you make room for what matters most, without spreading yourself too thin. 20 2

Tech tags: expo.io, gemini, react-native. Hardware mentions: quest.

Sponsor opt-ins: [MLH] Best Use of Gemini API, Neon: Best Use of Neon, Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack. Submitted to: Knight Hacks IX. HTTP: 200.

Story evidence excerpt: What it does Fobo is a personal capacity planner. It shows when you're full, not just when you're free, and helps you decide what to do about it. It learns you, then learns alongside you. Fobo starts with a few gentle questions about how you recharge, when you like to head home, and what makes you feel like yourself again. After each event, a quick this-or-that ("Which plan cost more?", "About the same", "Can't remember") sharpens its picture of what energizes you and what drains you. Every question has a skip option, because we always offer an out. It shows your week as stacks. Each day is a stack of cut-paper scraps, one scrap per plan. The height of a scrap is its energy cost, and its color is the kind of plan: work, social, obligation, or enjoy. Each type has its own cut edge, so the stack reads without color alone (snipped corner, wavy cut, pinking shears, three scallops). Past

Exact serious-game and pathfinding/face fit is less explicit.</p></div></div><div class="rank "><span class="number">36</span><div><b><a href="https://devpost.com/software/reefroute" target="_blank" rel="noopener">ReefRoute</a></b><p>Reef-avoiding routes are a relevant environmental simulation; real navigation accuracy and user validation remain open.</p></div></div></details><small><a href="https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?prize_filter%5Bprizes%5D%5B%5D=107094&amp;page=1">Verified sponsor membership census</a> · Lower ranks are weak-confidence triage.</small></article></div></section><section id="rivals"><h2>The rivals to know by name</h2><div class="grid"><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/understory-gj3in2" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Understory</a></h3><p>The closest architecture rival: VR survey rover, SLAM, Nav2 and open plant models; staged surveys and past power/network problems temper confidence.</p><small>Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Maps as it drives: a 2D lidar builds a floor plan live with SLAM, and the robot plans its own survey points on that map. Captures 360° &quot;bubbles&quot;: at each survey point the robot spins in place, stopping every 30° to take 12 photos, each tagged with its heading. Finds and names plants: an open-vocabulary detector finds plant-like objects, and a biology model names the species and flags it as invasive or native. Pins each plant on the map: the plant&#x27;s position in the image plus the lidar range in that direction places it on the floor plan, with a cropped photo saved for each pin. VR walkthrough: a Meta Quest app shows a tabletop miniature of the map with the robot, bubbles, and pins. Pick a bubble and you are standing inside it, with the plants marked around you. Before and after: survey once, remove the invasives, survey again, and the app shows which plants are gone and which remain (&quot;7 invasive → 1 remaining&quot;). Voice commands: hold a button on the Quest controller and say &quot;start survey&quot;, &quot;take a picture&quot;, or &quot;come back home&quot;. Speech is transcribed on the robot&#x27;s own net</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Raspberry Pi 5 rover, table-side Jetson Orin Nano, ESP32, RPLIDAR C1, Brio camera, mecanum chassis</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/permanence" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Permanence</a></h3><p>A real shell-game reveal tests 3D memory directly; reported 7 recorded and 3 live successes plus custom quantization make a formidable rival.</p><small>Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Permanence gives a robot a persistent 3D memory of objects. The rover scans the table by itself, identifies the objects, and keeps track of where they are as they get hidden or leave the camera&#x27;s view. Our clearest demonstration is a shell game. Show the robot colored balls, cover them with cups, shuffle the cups, then reveal. Permanence tracks which cup holds each hidden ball and checks its answer against the real reveal. It called the right cup in 7 of 7 recorded games and 3 of 3 live. You can also: Talk to it. &quot;Hey rover, the cup next to the green ball&quot;: a 3D vision-language model picks the object from the scan and shows how confident it is. See its memory. A live 3D dashboard and an x-ray display on XREAL glasses show hidden objects through the cups covering them. See what&#x27;s seen versus remembered, including how long each object has been out of sight. Send it to a remembered target. &quot;Hey rover, drive&quot;: the rover plans around the other cups and goes to its target, even when nothing on camera shows where the ball is. The cups are a demonstration, not the whole ide</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Jetson Orin Nano 8 GB, LiDAR iPhone via Record3D, Arduino Uno, L298N, XREAL glasses</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/vigil-1fx8gp" target="_blank" rel="noopener">VIGIL</a></h3><p>Custom flight electronics and live thermal detection are strong SAR hardware; stable hover and camera tests are not an integrated autonomous search flight.</p><small>Hugging Face, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does VIGIL is a low cost thermal search-and-rescue drone prototype designed to help operators locate people when visibility is limited. The drone cost under $500 to build, which is nearly 1/10th the price of the nearest competitor. It combines a thermal camera, person-detection AI, and a ground-station dashboard. Operators can watch live thermal footage with tracked person detection, review each suspected person using the exact image that triggered the detection, and see estimated locations when enough telemetry is available. Operators can also add or remove person markers manually and create search routes. Locally saved maps and terrain elevation data allow prepared mission areas to remain available without internet access. How we built it Lepton 3.5 → ESP32-S3 (Wi-Fi) → ground computer (YOLO26 detection + tracking) → Socket.IO → web dashboard Flight controller (INAV) → ExpressLRS → radio → telemetry bridge → web dashboard We built VIGIL as four connected systems: flight control, thermal capture, AI processing, and the operator interface. The drone uses a custom carrie</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Custom STM32 flight board, FLIR Lepton 3.5, XIAO ESP32-S3, ExpressLRS radio, GPS</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/mimisis" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Mimisis</a></h3><p>Stereo neural hand tracking drives a real arm; reported latency and pick-and-place results make the hard robotics work easy to assess.</p><small>Hugging Face, Full Sail</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Mimesis is an end-to-end, vision-based teleoperation system for the SO-ARM101. It works as follows: Hand Tracking: Two consumer webcams and the WiLoR neural network find 21 keypoints on each hand. We triangulate them into a 3D hand pose. Two-Hand Control: The right hand moves the gripper through 3D space. Twisting the left hand rotates the wrist, and making a fist closes the gripper. Motion Control: A custom inverse-kinematics solver turns the hand pose into joint angles. A 50 Hz driver moves the arm with safety checks on each joint. Research Ready: Sessions can be recorded and replayed exactly. A ROS 2 interface lets researchers plug in their own trackers, controllers or AI policies. How we built it Mimesis combines stereo computer vision, Kalman filtering, inverse kinematics and real-time servo control, connected through ROS 2. The Hardware: An SO-ARM101 arm with six Feetech STS3215 servos, plus two Elgato Facecam MK.2 webcams at 1080p and 60 fps. The Vision Pipeline: A YOLO hand detector and WiLoR (a vision-transformer hand model) run in PyTorch. An IMM Kalman</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: SO-ARM101 with six STS3215 servos, two Elgato Facecam MK.2 webcams, local or AMD cloud GPU</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/atlas-q4wypt" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Atlas</a></h3><p>Jetson memory, cited answers and multiple connectors have quantified soak and throughput claims; training on AMD is separated from edge runtime.</p><small>Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Atlas is one everyday tool: a personal AI computer (an NVIDIA Jetson) that connects to your devices and apps, remembers what happens across them, and helps with your day. Plugs into anything. Every device or app is a connector: reads sync into Atlas&#x27;s memory, and anything that writes to the outside world waits for your tap. Today: a camera, the web (search and Reddit through a real browser), Canvas, your Outlook calendar, GroupMe, the campus site, your watch&#x27;s health data, and voice on your phone. Smart lights, a gate or a car plug in the same way. Remembers. Everything it hears about becomes events in an append-only log: its memory. The camera is one source: detection, tracking and zones run on the Jetson, and faces are blurred before anything is stored. Answers in plain English, with evidence. &quot;What happened at our table overnight?&quot; &quot;Where did I leave my keys?&quot; &quot;What&#x27;s due this week?&quot; Every answer cites the episode, keyframe, message or link it came from. If Atlas doesn&#x27;t know, it says so. Alerts and acts, with consent. A package left at the door buzzes your phone. A</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Jetson, quest, camera, projector</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/hearthwatch-0bkwp4" target="_blank" rel="noopener">HearthWatch</a></h3><p>A trained posture model on a 2 GB Nano connects to nurse response; reported locked-test metrics and live 13 FPS strengthen execution evidence.</p><small>Hugging Face, Full Sail, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does HearthWatch connects local camera observations and care check-ins to a nurse response workflow: Possible Fall Detection: Our own posture model, trained by us (using timed labels from Hugging Face&#x27;s OmniFall) and running on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano, labels everyone in view as upright, sitting, or on the floor. Prolonged floor tagging notifies a nurse to check in. Gesture Help Requests: Recognize a held fist as a request for assistance, with a manual help button as another option. Nurse Subscriptions: Let nurses select rooms and alert categories, receive updates, and accept responsibility for a response. Appointment Reminders: Notify the care team when a patient&#x27;s arrival is not confirmed within 15 minutes of a scheduled appointment. Camera Health: Flag disconnected sources, stale observations, and missing visibility. Simulation and Replay: Explore resident activities and staged incidents in a 3D environment where residents and staff navigate with pathfinding. Real people seen by the Jetson appear as live avatars beside clearly labelled simulated residents. How we built it</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Jetson Nano, quest, Camera</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/omnibelt" target="_blank" rel="noopener">OmniBelt</a></h3><p>Custom belt and ring integrate depth, object memory and last-meter guidance; substantial sensor fusion with a clear wearer interaction.</p><small>Hugging Face, Full Sail, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Say What happens &quot;Belt, find my wallet.&quot; Finds it, walks you there around obstacles, then hands over to the ring at arm&#x27;s length. The ring pulses faster as your hand gets closer and gives a solid buzz when you&#x27;re on it. &quot;Belt, where did I leave my keys?&quot; Answers from memory (&quot;on the table behind you, to your right, about 2 meters away&quot;), then asks &quot;Want me to take you there?&quot; &quot;This is my wallet.&quot; Hold it up and the belt learns your wallet, not just any wallet. &quot;Belt, find me a seat.&quot; Finds a chair nobody is sitting in and walks you to it. &quot;Belt, take me to the door.&quot; Walks you to the nearest door and buzzes when you arrive. &quot;Belt, what&#x27;s in front of me?&quot; A short spoken description, obstacles first. Walk mode Dodges obstacles from ankle to head height in real time, fully offline. &quot;Stop.&quot; Stops everything, any time. An iPhon</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Jetson Orin Nano Super, ESP32, iPhone 17, LiDAR, camera</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/veil-8xerbq" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Waymaker</a></h3><p>Offline Jetson vision, ultrasonic haptics and local Q&amp;A have a clear wearer benefit; loose wiring and broad testing remain limitations.</p><small>Hugging Face, NVIDIA, Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Waymaker is a backpack wearable for blind and low-vision people that runs fully offline on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano. Feel obstacles: two ultrasonic sensors and a chest camera drive vibration motors under the left and right backpack straps. Pulses speed up as obstacles get closer, and turn steady when it&#x27;s time to stop. Understand the street: an on-device YOLO11n model tracks people, bikes, poles, and other obstacles, and predicts whether each one is actually on a collision course. A second model reads pedestrian crossing signals and announces when they change. Safety first: crossing-signal alerts interrupt the assistant mid-sentence, and the motors never wait on the AI. If the camera fails, the ultrasonic sensors keep working. It automates the part of triage a person currently has to do on foot: finding the patient, getting to them, and taking a reading. In autonomous mode it sweeps the room looking for a patient — not just any person, but specifically someone in a medical gown, identified by combining YOLO person detection with a calibrated color signature. A person alone isn&#x27;t enough and the gown color alone isn&#x27;t enough; both have to pass before it commits. Once it has a lock, it drives toward them, steering around obstacles with lidar and slowing as it closes, then holds position on arrival. From there it switches from navigating to monitoring. Using facial landmarks it tracks eye closure against a baseline it calibrates on the spot for that specific person, and reports PERCLOS — the share of a rolling 60-second window the patient&#x27;s eyes were closed. PERCLOS is the standard clinical measure of drowsiness, and it&#x27;s the earliest honest signal you can get from a camera that a patient is fading. Providing Environmental Adaptability - Using tools such hazardous gas modules for detecting site hazards as radar for detecting humans through dust. ## How we built it We were able to localize our robot controls and storage by keeping the Jetson fully on our robot, connected to a battery source and our ESP32. In addition, ma</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: Jetson Orin Nano, ESP32, iPhone, camera, radar</p></details></article><article class="card"><h3><a href="https://devpost.com/software/pulse-cpnfut" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Pulse</a></h3><p>A handheld dual-radar prototype captures target positions; vital-to-target association and through-wall rescue performance remain bounded.</p><small>Encompass</small><details><summary>Published evidence and hardware</summary><p>What it does Pulse combines two radar sensors to give responders a clearer picture of who may be nearby. It combines AR tracking, step counting and the building&#x27;s walls to work out your position. It also reads room-number signs (&quot;121&quot;, &quot;107A&quot;) through the camera to pin down exactly where you are. Guides you in AR. A route on the floor and a turn banner tell you where to go next. It recalculates if you go a different way. Talks with you. Say &quot;take me to room 107&quot; or &quot;where&#x27;s the bathroom?&quot; to set a route. Doorway reads each turn aloud, so you can keep your eyes up instead of on your phone. Lets you browse the building. Search or scroll a directory of rooms, bathrooms, elevators, stairs and entrances. A floor map you can zoom and a minimap show where you are and where y</p><p class="muted">Hardware mentioned: camera, GPS</p></details></article></div></section><section id="pitch"><h2>Make the mission easy to verify</h2><div class="card focus"><p class="lede">Drive → find a person → ask about the view → open the room → click the original sighting.</p><p>Use one short, continuous mission in the pitch: show the rover and its onboard Jetson; drive to a person; show the live detection and a grounded local answer; open that sighting in the room and click through to the original image. If reconstruction is prebuilt, label it as a recorded run and state the bounded timing. Present only the portions that work in the available demo. Show a repeatable live movement/perception slice before adding optional features. This is a presentation recommendation, not a request to change control behavior or add runtime checks.</p><p>Lead NVIDIA with onboard compute, Encompass with capture-to-evidence, Hugging Face with the models’ concrete roles and observed usefulness, and Full Sail with the trainee’s goal and debrief. Do not tell every room the same six-minute feature list. No external submission, post, like, comment, code change, push or PR was made by this audit.</p></div><div class="grid" style="margin-top:18px"><article class="card"><h3>Farsight’s advantages</h3><p><b>Breadth with a coherent mission</b><br>A responder drives, finds a person, asks about the view, then reviews a spatial record. The components can tell one story rather than compete for attention.</p><p><b>Measured spatial capture plus learned appearance</b><br>LiDAR/pose and original images can ground the room and sightings; the splat adds visual context. Understory’s public roadmap still puts full Gaussian-splat sites in future work.</p><p><b>Useful provenance</b><br>A face pin opening its actual boxed camera frame is more persuasive than an unexplained map dot or a generated medical note.</p><p><b>Substantial edge integration</b><br>Vision, navigation, streaming, motor commands and local voice share a constrained onboard device; explain the real partition clearly.</p></article><article class="card"><h3>What the field exposes</h3><p><b>Reliability can erase breadth</b><br>Functionality explicitly asks whether the project reliably works end to end. Power instability or a long restart can outweigh several impressive subsystems.</p><p><b>No equally compact truth test</b><br>Permanence can hide a ball and reveal the answer. Farsight needs an equally obvious person → location → original-frame demonstration.</p><p><b>Reconstruction timing is narrower than a headline</b><br>The saved-sample build is useful engineering evidence, but not a live end-to-end turnaround receipt.</p><p><b>Limited field validation</b><br>No verified responder study, broad SAR localization test, repeated physical mission benchmark or clinical validation was established in this audit.</p><p><b>Full Sail fit needs a training outcome</b><br>VR presence and Nav2 alone do not make a serious training simulation. Demonstrate an actual learner task and debrief; describe unshown scoring as unverified.</p><p><b>Open AI is integrated, not uniquely trained</b><br>Several rivals contribute model evaluation, custom quantization, training or specialist pipelines; model-name breadth alone will not win Hugging Face.</p></article></div><div class="warning" style="margin-top:18px"><b>The “two minutes” claim has a boundary</b><p>114 s was a saved 60 s / 119-photo local sample, reusing a longer run’s LiDAR seed. 124 s adds an idle allowance and local-copy stand-in, excluding real Jetson transfer and browser loading. Face-pin interaction is browser evidence; immersive Quest parity remains future work.</p><a href="https://devpost.com/software/farsight-t4qyli">Farsight’s published limitations</a></div></section><section id="method"><h2>What this audit proves</h2><div class="card"><p>Audited all 9 gallery pages: 24 projects on each of pages 1–8 and 20 on page 9, totaling 212 unique URLs. Page 9’s next control points to “#”, establishing the end of pagination. Read all gallery cards; retrieved and skimmed 134 plausible or same-track project pages, all HTTP 200. Every one of the 114 unique entrants in the four requested sponsor pools is included, plus 20 other plausible/general contenders. The remaining 78 received card screening only.</p><p>Track membership comes from the event’s exact sponsor filters, paginated to the end: Hugging Face 78 (4 pages), Full Sail 36 (2), NVIDIA 21 (1), Encompass 43 (2). These are opt-in counts, not proof of eligibility, judging attendance, or winners. The public project pages’ “Submitted to” sections show the event, not a complete per-prize list; Farsight’s story separately names all four tracks. I therefore use the filtered galleries for verified prize membership and do not pretend it was visible under every project’s “Submitted to”. A second pass also paginated all 19 sponsor-prize filters, so the census and dossiers include every publicly filter-visible sponsor opt-in, not only the four ranked pools. General prizes do not require a separate sponsor opt-in.</p><p>Evidence is first-party Devpost prose, embedded/linked demo availability and published metrics. I did not independently replay every video, inspect rival repositories, operate rival demos or observe judging. Numbers attributed to teams are self-reported. A video link is not proof that every claimed feature works. This is a complete gallery/target-track census and a public-evidence ranking audit, not a physical validation audit.</p><p>The live event rubric is Originality, Technical Understanding, Functionality, Design and Wow Factor. I combine those with impact and exact sponsor fit; no official numerical weights are published on that page. Integrated functioning and narrow checkable claims are preferred to long feature lists. Order beyond the leading ~15 is especially uncertain; the complete lists provide triage, not meaningful one-place precision. Entry age, likes, comments and page position are not scoring inputs. No official winners were used or inferred.</p><p><a href="https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/">Live event rubric and prizes</a> · <a href="https://knight-hacks-ix.devpost.com/submissions/search?utf8=%E2%9C%93">Complete gallery</a></p><p class="muted">All 19 sponsor-prize filters were paginated to verify the exact opt-ins. Public “Submitted to” sections show the event, not a full sponsor list. No rival video was independently replayed or hardware operated.</p></div></section><section id="catalog"><details><summary>All 19 sponsor-prize entry counts</summary><p>[MLH] Best Use of ElevenLabs: 35</p><p>[MLH] Best Use of Gemini API: 47</p><p>[MLH] Best Use of Tiger Data: 3</p><p>[MLH] Best Use of Solana: 1</p><p>[MLH] Best Use of Presage: 2</p><p>[MLH] Best Use of Vultr: 7</p><p>[MLH] Best Domain Name from GoDaddy Registry: 31</p><p>Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI: 78</p><p>Full Sail: Pathways &amp; Perception, A Serious Games &amp; Simulation Challenge: 36</p><p>NVIDIA: Best Use of Jetson Nano: 21</p><p>Encompass: Data Capturing Hardware: 43</p><p>BNY: Financial Services Challenge: 14</p><p>NextEra Energy: SPOT Management Dashboard: 11</p><p>OneEthos: Innovation for Good: 34</p><p>AMD: Best Use of AMD Technologies: 23</p><p>Databricks: Best Use of Databricks: 25</p><p>Neon: Best Use of Neon: 29</p><p>Figma: Best Design: 64</p><p>Adobe: Prettiest Hack: 64</p></details><h2>All 212 projects, searchable</h2><p class="muted">Every gallery card is accounted for. Tech and hardware not verified.</p></article><article data-search="the aifa (american infrastructure fixing agent) i learned about how computer vision yesterday from the guys at #nexteraenergy and decided try the spot challenge because at the workshop i instantly thought of a moonshot agent for good - #oneethos [mlh] best use of gemini api,nextera energy: spot management dashboard,oneethos: innovation for good quest, eog"><b><a href="https://devpost.com/software/the-aifa-american-infrastructure-fixing-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener">The AIFA (American Infrastructure Fixing Agent)</a></b><p>I learned about how Computer Vision yesterday from the guys at #NextEraEnergy and decided try the spot challenge because at the workshop I instantly thought of a moonshot agent for good - #OneEthos</p><small>[MLH] Best Use of Gemini API, NextEra Energy: SPOT Management Dashboard, OneEthos: Innovation for Good</small><details><summary>Retrieved project dossier</summary><p>Tech tags: cloudflare, gemini, google, python</p><p>Hardware mentioned: quest, eog</p><p class="evidence">What it does The AIFA is a tool that helps surface and prioritize infrastructure issues so public officials, engineers, and community groups can make clearer decisions with the information they already have. It pulls together publicly available data on roads, bridges, weather risk, and related conditions, then highlights which problems appear most urgent based on safety, community impact, and available context. Users can ask straightforward questions such as “Which bridges in this area should we look at first after heavy rain?” or “Where should limited maintenance dollars go to reduce the biggest risks?” and receive a prioritized list with supporting details and suggested next steps. The goal is not to replace engineers or policymakers. It is to help them spend less time hunting for information and more time acting on the highest-impact issues. How we built it I focused on making the tool useful for people who need answers, not on building the most complex system possible. Team up, coordinate, and don’t get tilted.</p><small>No sponsor-prize opt-in found</small><details><summary>Retrieved project dossier</summary><p>Tech tags: css3, html5, javascript, node.js, three.js, typescript, websockets</p><p>Hardware mentioned: quest</p><p class="evidence">What it does Tilted is a cooperative multiplayer physics game where players use the physical tilt of their phones to control one shared board. Each phone sends a 2D motion vector representing the player’s tilt. The host combines the players’ inputs into a single team vector, and that resulting direction tilts the game board. The marble then moves naturally through the physics simulation. The core idea is: $$[ \mathbf{T} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{v}_i ]$$ where each $$(\mathbf{v}_i)$$ is one player’s input vector and $$(\mathbf{T})$$ is the team’s combined input. When players coordinate, the marble moves strongly in the intended direction. When they fight each other, the board levels out and the marble slows down. It finds the most relevant parts of the policy, writes an answer using only those parts, and says when the answer is not in the document. Users can download the full policy from the app to check the answer themselves. How I built it Extract: read the 19-page policy PDF with PyMuPDF and removed the repeated page headers. Chunk: split the text into chunks of 1200 characters with 150 overlap, using LangChain&#x27;s RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter. Embed and index: turn each chunk into a vector with the BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 embedding model and store them in a FAISS index. Retrieve: embed the question the same way and fetch the 3 closest chunks. By default, FAISS ranks chunks by distance, where a smaller distance means a closer match: $$d(\mathbf{q}, \mathbf{c}) = \lVert \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{c} \rVert_2$$ Answer: send the question and the retrieved chunks to a local qwen2.5:7b model through Ollama, with rules to answer only from the excerpts and not to invent policy names or numbers. Most people wander a grocery store, double back for forgotten items, and cross the store more than once. I wanted a tool that takes a grocery list and gives the shortest possible walk through the store, with clear guidance the whole way. Core loop Type your list. Enter items in plain words. The app understands typos (&quot;bannana&quot;), plurals, and common names (&quot;oj&quot; for juice, &quot;tp&quot; for toilet paper), and tells you when the store doesn&#x27;t carry something. Get the best route. The app finds the order to visit the aisles that keeps your total walking distance as short as possible, then draws a glowing line on the floor with an arrow pointing the way. Follow and shop. When you reach an aisle, a prompt asks if you want to pick up each item. Picked-up items are checked off your list and appear in your cart. Stay on track. The Oracle judges a project&#x27;s chances of winning using a two stage classifier. A text model estimates how &quot;winner-like&quot; the project write up is. Challenges we ran into We lost a lot of time coming up with ideas and didn&#x27;t start until the second day. This put us on a pretty substantial time crunch and taught us the importance of preparing some ideas before hand. With this, a person can order from a phone, browse 18 UCF campus shops and their menus in the iOS app, build a cart, and check out with the current location becoming the drop-off point. The whole point is that the robot comes to the customer. The order goes to the robot, which drives itself along sidewalks using cameras, depth, lidar, and GPS. It stops for obstacles and people, and treats crosswalks carefully. Live tracking is also available, following the robot on a map in real time, with order status from &quot;placed&quot; through &quot;on the way&quot;, &quot;arrived,&quot; and &quot;delivered&quot;. In contact between the person and robot, a secure hand-off takes place. Each order gets a PIN. Enter it on the robot&#x27;s touchscreen to release your food. Five wrong tries lock it for 10 minutes. There are apps for every role: customers order and track, shop staff accept orders and send the robot, and operators see the robot&#x27;s position and battery, and can emergency-stop or resume it from their phone. Users gaze at flickering tiles associated with messages, and generative AI is used to bring meaning to their responses. How we built it Visual Frequencies: The human brain will attempt to replicate visual frequencies from flashing lights with Steady-State Visually Evoked Potentials (SSVEPs). We use these to associate them with meaningful messages and allow users to communicate with a glance. EEG Acquisition: Electrodes placed at the occipital lobe receive SSVEPs that are emitted when the user is exposed to specific visual frequencies associated with messages. AI Interpreters: Two AI models, a TRCA model and a model using the EEGNet neural network, calibrate themselves to the user’s brain waves and then interpret their SSVEPs into meaningful messages or commands. Challenges we ran into Training data was scarce, and the public SSVEP datasets we found varied in experiment type and had small inconsistencies with our needs, making them hard to unify. Our platform also provides equipment histories, performance analytics, interactive facility maps, and simulated scenarios involving extreme weather and obstacles. How we built it We built Watchdog using Next.js, React, TypeScript, and Tailwind CSS, with the interface designed in Figma. Our custom simulation engine uses A pathfinding and Canvas 2D* to animate SPOT navigating inspection routes. The backend uses Node.js, Express, and Neon PostgreSQL to manage inspection records, sensor readings, anomalies, and operator decisions. We also explored AI integration using a Hugging Face summarization model, allowing users to generate draft mission summaries with validation checks against inspection records. The application is deployed using Vercel and Railway. Two-Hand Control: The right hand moves the gripper through 3D space. Twisting the left hand rotates the wrist, and making a fist closes the gripper. Motion Control: A custom inverse-kinematics solver turns the hand pose into joint angles. A 50 Hz driver moves the arm with safety checks on each joint. Research Ready: Sessions can be recorded and replayed exactly. A ROS 2 interface lets researchers plug in their own trackers, controllers or AI policies. How we built it Mimesis combines stereo computer vision, Kalman filtering, inverse kinematics and real-time servo control, connected through ROS 2. The Hardware: An SO-ARM101 arm with six Feetech STS3215 servos, plus two Elgato Facecam MK.2 webcams at 1080p and 60 fps. The Vision Pipeline: A YOLO hand detector and WiLoR (a vision-transformer hand model) run in PyTorch. It automates the part of triage a person currently has to do on foot: finding the patient, getting to them, and taking a reading. In autonomous mode it sweeps the room looking for a patient — not just any person, but specifically someone in a medical gown, identified by combining YOLO person detection with a calibrated color signature. A person alone isn&#x27;t enough and the gown color alone isn&#x27;t enough; both have to pass before it commits. Once it has a lock, it drives toward them, steering around obstacles with lidar and slowing as it closes, then holds position on arrival. From there it switches from navigating to monitoring. Using facial landmarks it tracks eye closure against a baseline it calibrates on the spot for that specific person, and reports PERCLOS — the share of a rolling 60-second window the patient&#x27;s eyes were closed. PERCLOS is the standard clinical measure of drowsiness, and it&#x27;s the earliest honest signal you can get from a camera that a patient is fading. Those who do not know how to code become empowered with a new innovative skill and those who already knew became even more efficient. But not all models are easily accessible. Some people cannot afford the rising token prices. Some companies have data that is far too sensitive to share over the internet with an LLM provider. In August of this year, Pinterest has reported over 92% savings in LLM transaction costs. Uber has reported 52% savings per AI agent session. Lyzr.ai, Axis, Factory, and OpenPipe are other companies that have reported similar results. Users can then ask follow up questions about the images they&#x27;ve uploaded. How we built it We built this app using a React-based frontend (Vite and Tailwind CSS) and a FastAPI backend that passes each user question through a LangChain RAG pipeline, which retrieves the most relevant passages from a ChromaDB vector database of survival documents and feeds them to Llama 3.2 3B running locally on Ollama. Type in a ticker RiskDiff pulls both filings straight from the SEC, finds the Risk Factors section, and splits it into individual risks. It then matches each risk to last year&#x27;s version, even if it was reworded or moved, so wording that changed slightly but means the same thing doesn&#x27;t show up as a new risk. What changed is always decided by fixed rules in code, so the same two filings give the same result every time. AI only helps with the matching and with short summaries that are clearly labeled. What it shows you A label for every risk factor: new risk, dropped risk, expanded, scaled back, major update, or essentially unchanged. For NVIDIA, 6 of 29 changed meaningfully, so you know which 23 you can skip. Potential to Actual: our favorite part. It flags a risk described as something that could happen last year that is described as something that did happen this year. New figures and new names: like the $4.5 billi</p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/embed/y2S0cWPg8sA?enablejsapi=1&amp;hl=en_US&amp;rel=0&amp;start=&amp;version=3&amp;wmode=transparent">Linked demo (not replayed)</a></p><small>Submitted to Knight Hacks IX · HTTP 200 · filter-verified sponsor membership.</small></details></article><article data-search="he can see us one virtual reality monster vs many pc goblins figma: best design,adobe: prettiest hack no capture hardware identified in skim."><b><a href="https://devpost.com/software/he-can-see-us" target="_blank" rel="noopener">He Can See Us</a></b><p>One Virtual Reality Monster VS Many PC Goblins</p><small>Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack</small><details><summary>Retrieved project dossier</summary><p>Tech tags: c#, github, unity</p><p>Hardware mentioned: No capture hardware identified in skim.</p><p class="evidence">1 About Our Game He Can See Us is a multiplayer survival game made in Unity where players must hide from a player playing as a monster using a virtual reality headset. Players on desktop run for refuge in a desperate attempt to shelter from the monster&#x27;s wrath! How we built it This project was built using the Unity Game Engine version 6000.4.9f1. For VR support we used Unity&#x27;s built in XR plugin and for online multiplayer we utilized Unity&#x27;s Netcode for GameObjects alongside Unity Relay. Finally, we used Libre Fracture 2.0, an open-source plug-in for destruction simulation, to procedurally generate pre-fractured models. This meant we didn&#x27;t have to calculate the destruction of meshes at runtime, which saved us a lot of performance. We modified the source code so that when the monster hit a building, only the area within a certain radius of the impact would be destroyed, rather than the entire building at once. Challenges we ran into A major challenge that we ran into throughout the development of the project was to allow cross platform multiplayer play between VR and PC. Waverio Studio is a browser DAW for beginners. Record vocals, describe the sound you want, and AI builds effects tailored for your voice.</p><small>Hugging Face: Best Use of Open Source AI</small><details><summary>Retrieved project dossier</summary><p>Tech tags: javascript, node.js, react, typescript</p><p>Hardware mentioned: No capture hardware identified in skim.</p><p class="evidence">What it does Waverio Studio is a Digital Audio Workstation where artists are able to place beats, record vocals, and utilize an AI assistant to help with the engineering of their vocals. This AI assistant reads a written description of the sound they want, along with measurements of their recording, and builds a custom preset they can still edit. How we built it This project uses a React and TypeScript interface to run on a web browser, and uses libraries such as Tone.js to run the audio locally through the Web Audio API. The browser stores data locally through the use of localStorage and IndexedDB, which are used to store sessions and clip audio. Beacon can recognize someone it has seen before, display their name or notes on the glasses, and record where and when they were encountered. Beacon also builds a map from the wearer’s movement and can guide them back to a person’s last known location. Full frames and face crops are stored privately so users can review and correct recognition results. How we built it We built the Android application in Kotlin using Meta’s Wearables Device Access Toolkit for camera streaming and glasses display output. Fleet trends: why missions fail, and whether performance is improving. What we found in the data: Completed doesn&#x27;t mean it worked. 21 of 26 completed missions hid a failed action. How to use it Visit society-sim.us on a desktop. Watch a game replay and step through the votes, accusations, and labeled lies. Take a seat to play against the AI models. The global leaderboard shows the best liars and wolf detectors. The research page lets you explore the results across games. How we built it The Python engine runs the game and checks each model&#x27;s structured replies. LiteLLM connects players served by Hugging Face, Google, and OpenAI. We also ran a self-hosted gpt-oss-20b player on AMD hardware. Neon stores game events, and Databricks holds the data and recomputes the metrics. A live camera feed is processed locally to detect and track people and portable objects such as backpacks. Instead of treating every frame independently, ThirdEye builds a persistent world state that remembers what entities have been seen and how they relate to one another. ThirdEye can currently detect and reason about events such as: people entering and leaving the scene restricted-zone entry dwelling in a monitored area occupancy and crowding backpacks and other portable objects associations between people and objects unattended objects objects moving from their previous location objects leaving the scene with a person last-seen locations and event history ThirdEye also includes event-triggered incident recording. How we built it Our generation worker runs Kandinsky 6.0 Video Lite, an open-weight world model available on Hugging Face. Target lock: with several people in view, it follows only you. Lost and found: if you leave the frame, it looks where you were heading instead of freezing. If fully lost, the drone will start looking in a 360-degree radius, Phone control center: a live map with drone support, the camera view, modes, simulators, the radius, and an emergency stop, all on a nicely designed GUI. How I built it This project consists of four technological layers, each one talking to the next: the brace measures your location and angle, a base station relays this information with 30-byte packets, the laptop decides what to do with</p><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/embed/pPoEXRLnYJc?enablejsapi=1&amp;hl=en_US&amp;rel=0&amp;start=&amp;version=3&amp;wmode=transparent">Linked demo (not replayed)</a></p><small>Submitted to Knight Hacks IX · HTTP 200 · filter-verified sponsor membership.</small></details></article><article data-search="fobo we all know fomo, but what about the fear of burning out? fobo helps you make room for what matters most, without spreading yourself too thin. [mlh] best use of gemini api,neon: best use of neon,figma: best design,adobe: prettiest hack quest"><b><a href="https://devpost.com/software/fobo-fear-of-burning-out" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Fobo</a></b><p>We all know FOMO, but what about the fear of burning out? Fobo helps you make room for what matters most, without spreading yourself too thin.</p><small>[MLH] Best Use of Gemini API, Neon: Best Use of Neon, Figma: Best Design, Adobe: Prettiest Hack</small><details><summary>Retrieved project dossier</summary><p>Tech tags: expo.io, gemini, react-native</p><p>Hardware mentioned: quest</p><p class="evidence">What it does Fobo is a personal capacity planner. It shows when you&#x27;re full, not just when you&#x27;re free, and helps you decide what to do about it. It learns you, then learns alongside you. Fobo starts with a few gentle questions about how you recharge, when you like to head home, and what makes you feel like yourself again. After each event, a quick this-or-that (&quot;Which plan cost more?&quot;, &quot;About the same&quot;, &quot;Can&#x27;t remember&quot;) sharpens its picture of what energizes you and what drains you. Every question has a skip option, because we always offer an out. It shows your week as stacks. Each day is a stack of cut-paper scraps, one scrap per plan. The height of a scrap is its energy cost, and its color is the kind of plan: work, social, obligation, or enjoy. Each type has its own cut edge, so the stack reads without color alone (snipped corner, wavy cut, pinking shears, three scallops). Past plans drain to a calming grey. Instead of a percentage, you read the whole day at a glance by how tall the stack is. Rest is never a gap, but always a character. Step What happens 🔥 Heat A hot plate stands in for a server and heats one side of the generators ❄️ Cold An ice bath keeps the other side cold ⚡ Generate Four thermoelectric generators turn that temperature difference into electricity 🔋 Store The current flows straight into a battery pack, no charging chip needed 📈 Track Every reading streams to a public dashboard anyone can open on their phone We picked the battery so its own voltage keeps the generators near their most efficient point.
